THE MOST NUTRITIOUZ.

• 41 °YO , E P

GRATEFUL-COMFORTING. , • •A. , 1;.%

3REAKFAST-SUPPER,

LADIES' -LETTER:

MY DEAR NELLIE.

Outdoor and evening coats are now often made to do duty for both purposes. One is of putty. coloured cloth, with deep sable collar, over an under-collar of woollen guipure, which falls nearly to the waist. The coat can be worn either open or closed. The bishop sleeves are set into deep cuffs of guipure. A pretty hat is in drap de feutre of the same colour, with natural-coloured ostrich feathers, the one underneath the brim fastened with a knot of black velvet. I saw a useful morning or travelling toque of grey drap de feutre, with a mauve-tinted panne rosette at the aide, fastened with a jet and strass cabochon. Another hat is of black felt, the upturned brim swathed with white chiffon and heavy black and white ostrich feathers drooping over the side, fastened with an antique silver ..n3.ment.

Lace gowns are again popular, and although their first cost is rather high, they certainly loos their value and wear well, especially in black They are generally made up over satin, but those of cream lace are sometimes lined first with chiffon, and then with gold tissue, both linings being quite separate from the lace over dress itself. This is a costly style of making, but the effect is ex- ceedingly handsome. Tambour-lace dress-pieces are very smart. One of the prettiest of gowns is of black tambour-lace with silver sequins pow- dered thickly over tho ground; another type has the design in cream or ticru on black net, while there is a great variety of patterns in all-white and all-cream. They need very little trimming; a few folds of net or chiffon round the top of the bodice, frills of the same to finish the sleeves (which should only just reach the elbow), and a pretty velvet or satin belt, are all the " extras " required to complete a stylish little hall-dress, although a shoulder-knot of flu as may be added if liked.

I For dancing-dresses nothinq, is in greater fAvour than the light and dainty c.-T, it net made up over satin, or, failing this, soft mercerised sateen. which hangs rather better than cheap glace. The net skirt is generally edged with two or more frills, sometimes bordered with narrow silver or gold galon or with white baby-ribbon, and the low, rounded neck of the bodice is prettily draped with folds of soft satin or panne, drawn iota a large loose rosette at the left side of the front. The sleeves generally reach to the elbows, where they are finished with frills, and a narrow gold or silver belt completes the dress. A rather smarter way of making the bodice of a net gown is to cover it with a lattice-work of white satin baby-ribbon, and to drape the neck with a white chiffon tichu edged with lace, the sleeves being of chiffon with lace frills. Or the bodice can be trimmed with a

large cluster of flowers—roses, wistaria, and orchids are particularly fashionable at the moment —tied with bows of wide, soft satin ribbon.

Never at any time was fashion more adaptable than at the present, or more suited to all sorts and conditions of wemen alike. There is no hard and fast rule to be observed, but so long as a garment be pleasing to the eye, it has a distinction whichi entitles it to be called fashionable, whether it comes strictly within the meaning of that classic term or not. There are modes to suit the divinely tall or the opposite, so that no woman need feel aggrieved that her particular style of beauty has not been duly catered for this season. But much care and forethought are needed if we would be apparelled consistently at all times, for multi. plicity of detail and endless elaboration of choice render it an increasingly difficult task to buy the right thing. A charming visiting or reception dress can be made of beaver-brown cloth strapped with silk of the same shade outlined with gold braid. The large guipure cellar is of new shape, and is laid on over black silk and edged with the gold braid. The bolero, sleeves, and three flounces are all trimmed to match each other with stitchings and strappings. The inner vest and unclersleeves are in green velvet, with cuffs and out vest of pale, putty-coloured cloth embroidered in pale colours. The swathed collar and tie are lace, and the belt is green velvet. An excellent bodice has a perfectly tight back, with the deep collar falling over it, and the neck is shown with only a small opening, but this can be lower, and the collar arranged to run lower down the fronts to form revers. There are small tucks back and front, and insertion lace runs up each front and rests on a full vest of chiffon. The sleeves can be quite long, with the lace medallions left transparent, or they can terminate at the elbow with a frill, and for summer wear a small chernisette and little full undersleeves will trans. form the gown into one entirely suitable for smart outdoor wear. . loving

WOMEN'S CHAI.

Buckingham Palace will next year present a striking contrast to its semi-deserted appearance of the roast half century. The alterations which have provica.e many noble suites of rooms, are nearing completion, and early in the New Year, the King and Queen Consort will take up their residence at the Palace, when in London. Marlborough House will then be handed over to the Prince and Princess of Wales, and, numerous as are the Royal Palaces in, and near, the Metropolis, their capa- city will be taxed to the utmost to provide accommoda- tion for the many Royal guests who will be invited to the Coronation. What with the drawing rooms and balls, the banquets and receptions, and garden parties, Buckingham Palace instead of being a deserted mansion will next year be the scene of a varied and continual disn'ay of Royal splendour. e-The Russian Court will be represented at the Corona- tion; by the Grand Duke Serge, the uncle of the Czar. The Grand Duchess, who is the second daughter of Princess Alice, is. by common consent, the most beauti- ful of all Queen Victoria's grand-children, a distinction however, which some think she should share with her younger sister the Czarina. At one time it was thought that the Princess Elizabeth—now the Grand Duchess Serge of Russia—would have married into the German Imperial Family, in which event she would now have been Empress of Germany, but Prince Bismarck was opposed to what he regarded as an English alliauce,and successfully kept the two cousins apart. The lily-of-the-valley, it seems, is to be the flower for the coming Coronation. It was the favourite flower of Queen Victoria, but many think that the rose would have been more appropriate to the historical event next year. There is never any difficulty in obtaining roses from our own gardens in the month of June, whereas the greater quantity of lily-of-the-valley blooms are im- ported from Holland and Germany. In view, however, of the large demard for the lily-of-the-valley, that is likely to spring up, our florists would do well to culti- vate the blooms during the coming season. Lady E Warwick, who has done so much to educate women in the arts of gardenieg and agriculture, has established a rural school of science at Dunmow, in Essex. Like Mr Meredith's old farmer in "Rhoda Fleming," the majority of those who live on the land have a rooted objection to " new feckled ways," but some knowlege of botany and chemistry is becoming more and more generally recegnised as a necessary qualification for successful farming. Lady Warwick goes so far as to say that" our agricultural and rural industries depend quite as much for their success on careful and scientific traininras the urban 'lndus- tries." The truth of this is fully recognised in Germany and the Countess of Warwick will render an immense service to the agricultural industry if, by her example, she could get the Government to serious y take up the question of rural scientific education. Many fashionable weddings are taking place this winter, that of the Duke of Hamilton and Miss Nina Poore, which was celebrated very quietly the other day. following close uponl the weddings of Miss Victoria Long, and Miss Muriel Brodrick, the daughters of two of our Cabinet Ministers. The new Duchess of Hamil- ton is a sister[nf Major Montagu Poore, who married in 1893, Lady Flora, the sister of the Duke of Hamilton. It is therefore the second marriage between the two families, the Duke and the Major now being trothers- ie-law twice over. Amongst other weddings which will be the occasion of a good deal of interest in Society circles, will be these of Lord Wicklow and Lady Gladys Hamilton, and of Lord Stavorciale and Lady Helen Stewart, both of which will be celebrated in London next month, when many people will be back in town for the opening of Parliament. Matrimonial troubles seem to be prevalent at several of the Courts of the smaller kingdoms of Europe.—To those of Servia, and Ilesse-Darmstadt. must now be added the Dutch Court where the differences between Queen Wilhelmina and her Consort, have recently been the subject of the most painful stories. That there has been some incompatibility of temper between the Royal couple has long been current gossip in Holland. The Queen who is but twenty one has a strong will of her own,and the position of Prince Consort is never an easy one, In the case of Prince Henry it has been made all the more difficult by the singular ungraciousness with which he has been received by the Dutch people on account of his German nationality. The woman who patronises the fashionable skating rinks, noes in for the pastime earnestly, and is not con- tent with anything short of figures, done in specified time and style. Consequently her skirt is business-like, being short and Dlain, though invariably lined and frilled with some daintily coloured silk, g,limpscs of which one catches as she swings and glides about. But here all plainness ends ; the little coatee, blouse, and headgear being of the smartest. Beautiful furs too are donned, and much jewellery. Among the supremely graceful on their skater may be mentiened, Lady Helen Vincent, Lady Cobs, Lady Archibald Campbell, and Mrs. George Cornwallis West. The latter, a past mis- tress of the art—is quite as enthusiastic about it, as she was three seasons back. .

It is interesting to observe the difference in the methods of clever and skilful skaters. One does the most comp,icatsd figures easily, gracefully, gently. Her shirts flow out prettily, there is never a hard or sharp angle to any motion. an is gliding and swinging, and absolutely easy. Another does the same things, but always with jerks, even her waltzing appears rough and energetic. The graceful skater never seems to exert herself at all. The ungraceful skater although equally clever, appears tofrequire an effort for every twist and turn she performs, and her skirt flies out as if in protest at this violent treat ment. and her arms are always in evidence, looking like eccentric signal boards. All belts and bands dip down weil in front, indeed some dip down PO considerably that the wearers appear to be cut of proportion. The ideal of the old Greek aculptors was length of limb and a short waiat, but the Idea of modern tailors and dressmakers is a tong wait, whether in proportion or out of proportion to the length of the limb!. In building riding habits, this long waist is more particularly the aim of the man milliner, and the consequence is, that many a rider who looks a I junn on horseback, turns out to be a squat little crea- ture when she withdraw her font from the stirrup, and slips to Mother Earth. It would surely be truer art to preserve some kind of proportion between the dimen- slope of the body and the limbs. Almost all coleured costumes show soma touch of white, this usually appearing in the vest, and being repeated in the hat. To be correct the headgear should he entirely in white, hut needless to say this fashion is a costly one. A very little of the British climate, at this season of the year, suffices to remove its first fresh- ness' and then all beauty and atyle is gone. We are evidently growing weary of the flat variety of hat, for many of the newest models are raised quite high on the hair. To the generality of women this latter style is mush more beentning.and as in the case of the hlouse,we shall possibly retain it. despite Darns Faehion's decrees. Books are favourite Christmas gifts, and when select- log for girls of about sixteen and upwards, Katherine Trman's " A Girl of Galway" should not be missed. The tale is most charmingly told, and with the poetic touch we always look fnr, from the writer, and never fail •to find. The hook (Ga.) is published in handsome guise, and gracefully pictured by John H Bacon. MADGE.

RHOS-ON-SEA, COLWYN BAY.

Colwyn Bay has the enviable reputation of having developed more rapidly than any other health resort in North Wales, its population in 1881 being only 2,500, whilc in 1901 it numbers 8,600. So largely has it in- creased, says the " Court Circular," that it may be said to have outgrown itself, for to-day Rhos-on-Sea, only a mile from the Colwyn Bay Railway Station, is a more than formidable rival, with every prospect of de- veloping even much more rapidly than the town whence it has sprung. This may sound somewhat optimistic, but there are good grounds for the prophesy. Rhos-on- Sea possesses all the attractions and ad vantages of Colwyn Bay, which have made that place as much sclight after, and a few others besides. Its pier is 1.500 feet long, and its golf links, which have the repu- tation of being unsurpassed by few in the whole of tbe United Kingdom, are on the spot. The scenic surroundings, both marine and mountain, are amongst the finest in the principality ; its close r, proximity to Little Ormea Head on the sea-board, and .the midst of many mountains, some of which nearly I approach in height the great Snowdon range, not very far distant ny rail, make Rhos-on-Sea, on account of its sheltered position, an ideal spot at the moment to win- , ter in. Much as the Antiquarian may revel in tbe Monastery of Rhos Fynach and the ancient Fishing Weir, the bulk of the general public will be more con- cerned as to the up-to-date accommodation obtainable at the leading hotel, and here is one of the reasons for my sanguineness as to the future of Rhos-on-Sea. The Rhos Abbey Hotel, with its seventy or eighty rooms not long since refurnished by Waring, is a building about which one cannot help being enthusiastic at the risk of what may seem to be thought exaggeration. No matter where you walk in this most modern of hostelries the thickness of the carpets imparts a sense of comfort and warmth which is most pleasurable at this season of the year, while the taste dinlayed in the furnishing of all the bedrooms is most noticeable, some of them being dainty in the extreme. Ap;•opos of the luxuriousness of the appointments I remember when describing the appointments of a hotel de luxe in the South, being told that " you cannot eat decorations." Quite so ; and you are not asked to do this at the Rhos Abbey Hotel, where the cuisine is and always will be a strong point while in the hands of its new proprietors, Mr and Mrs P C Meier, both of whom hail from " The Windsor," the leading betel of Glasgow. Mr Meier was asaistant manager there for about five years, and Mrs Meier is the eldest daughter of Mr A M Thieni,preprietor of " The Windsor and the celebrated Peebles Hydro Hotel ; consequently, in such experienced and popular hands.the success of the Rhos Abbey Hotel is a foregone conclusion.

HORSES AND MEN.

" The horse is a nobul animil. It has four legs—on at each corner—a head at one end and a tale at the othe The head is opposite the tale, except when the anima turns round, when the tale is opposit the head." This' is what the schoolboy wrote of the horse. Old Mayor, author of a School Primer extensively used early in the Nineteenth Century, was hardly able to tell us more. Yet Mayor wrote in the Horse Age,previous to that Age of Steam which is now verging into the Age of Elec- tricity. A physician of great eminence writes :—" Our race- horses are trained so young that they soon are unet for the race-course. As with horses, so with men. A man used to be satisfied to have earned a competency at 60; now he strains to retire at 45 with a fortune. As with men, EO with race-horses ; the difficulty is to keep up their appetite and digestion." With unimpaired diges- tion the horse is enduring and fleet, the man fit to sus- tain cheerfully the extremes of fortune. Mr. Alfred Barnes, of Albert-read, Keynsham, near Bristol, says ;---" That world-famed remedy, Seigel's Syrup, has been a priceless boon to me. Thanks to its wonderfnl power, except for slight lameness, I ran as strong as ever, though 62. When first indigestion laid hold of me, everything went wrong. Often I was seized with giddiness and trembling, and had to grasp EICEIIe support to keep from falling. My appetite failed, and instead of that feeling of comfort and contentment which follows a good meal well digested, which I used to experience, there was a gnawing pain in my chest as if I VMS about to be sick, though relief sOdom cams that way. I couldn't sleep well, and consequently was weary ana worn out during the day. After a fashion I managed to get through my business ; but it was real hard work. For eight years I continued like this, never really well or free from pain. Many were the medicines I tried, but none of them did me any geed. Indigestion was sapping my strength, arid mak- ing an cld man of me before my time. It had such a grip ame that I feared nothing would shift it ; but it has gone now, and it departure dates from the time I began to take Seigel's Syrup. After taking the medi- cine for a week or two, my condition was immensely improved. It sharpened my appetite and made fool agree with me. I became bright and cheerful and feet younger. I thankfully acknowledge what Seigel's Syrup has done for me, and my experience may be pub- lisbed in any way." Such is the signed statement ef Mr. AlFred Barne2, a man well-known and esteemed in Keyesham. Mr. Barnes's case clear and unmistakable as it is, can be paralleled by hundreds of others throughout the United Kingdom. A QUEEN'S SOLDIER.

B i WALTER WOOD,

Author of "Barrack and Battlefield," "Famous British Warships," "Through Battle to Pro- motion," "A Captain in the Fusiliers," "A Soldier of the Legion," &c.

CHAPTER XIX. THE TROOPER GOES TO THE BASE.

When it was known that Mark was to go to the Base Yeomanry Hospital there arose a considerable number of men who knew all about it, and were anxious to give him details as to the treatment he might expect there and the sort of institution by which he would be tended and brought back to health. An infantryman, who had access to him, assured him that the hospital was a foretaste of Paradise; that dwellers within its precincts fed on the rarest luxuries, and that his word could be taken for it that champagnb and oysters were a steeple dish. He made it clear, moreover, that the camp was lighted by electricity, and that its appliances and appeintments generally were such as no Emperor's palace could command, although he confessed to no more knowledge of kingly dwellings than imagination gave him. A brief examination having satisfied Mark that the soldier spoke from hearsay and fancy, and not from personal observation and experience, he dis- abused his mind of the vision, and listened more willingly to a man who really had beeri to the hospital, and could swear that while there was no luxury there was everything the wounded or sick soldier needed, and that so far as skill and care could save a man he was in no danger of being lost. According to him, it was much more desirable to be inside the hospital than outside. "Now,". said the communicant, engagingly, "there's your own case. Your leg's in a bad way, bad to begin with by reason of the bullet bein' a Martini an' not a Mauser. On top of that there's your sufferin's and privations up till now. You're no more good for fightin'. I could tell that with half an eye, even if I 'adn't been hospital 'orderly at 'ome. You go to the base hospital. What 'appens ? You go into the operatin' theaytre with your leg, you ' come out without it. An' the wonderful part is that you don't see an' you don't know. Now, I've seen an' done a few things in any time. Many's the post-mortem—p.in.—l've made, practically by myself, that the doctor's got all the credit for, many's the operation I've seen; an' I can tell you I've seen no theaytre like the base hospital theaytre, an' no operations done as they do 'em there. It's what I should describe as a nice, clean, agreeable way they 'axe of doing things. All I can 'ope is that if I should be knocked over again I shall go back to that identical same place." "And you say you've been through all—all the other ? " said Mark, to whom the soldier's description had proved amazingly and horribly fascinating. A was quite possible that this was a foretaste of what he mat himself pass through. "Bather," answered the ex-orderly, proudly. "One bullet through the lungs an' another through the foot. I went through the complete mill, inaludin' the X-rays. You wouldn't under- stand what that was even if I told you. It's a bit of a mystery to me, I can tell you, an' I've seen most that's goin' in the way o' medical things,. but it's a machine that lets the doctor see through you. You look a bit stunned, but it's true. I've looked through a chap an' seen 'is 'cart beat, an' 'is inside rise an' fall. That's fact."

"It's a bit tall, Grant, isn't it ? " asked Mark, remembering how he had just suffered by reason of the imaginative efforts of aWell-meaning informant. "It's Gospel," insisted Grant, exasperated that his word should be doubted. "Dash it I • Don't I know? 'Aven't I seen it? I can • tell you don't believe me. Do you believe me when I tell you that the hospital'll accommodate a thousand patients, that it's so well fitted up that there's actually a fire-engine on the preMises, and that there's a church to go to an' a parson that edits a paper an' brings it out, as well as preaches, an' that he's a rattlin' good chap? Do you believe all that?"

"Yes," said Mark, rather unwillingly. "But you see I've been had by the other fellow so I felt a bit dubious. You've told me of the I:orrore of the place." "Not what I call'- harrora,l-said the 'eol, _ _ correctingly. "I shouldn't put it quite as strong as that."

Idler, _ I

"Very well, then, the glum side of the business," continued Mark. "Nor yet that," observed his companion. "You wait till you've seen as much as I've seen an' you'll think different. Of course "—he said this with lofty tolerance—"l quite understand 'ow things are.. You see, you're only a Yeoman. It isn't as if you'd been a real soldier when you came out an' ad been through the mill as I 'ave. Now, when you come to think of it, there's a good deal more difference than you'd think at first, if you understand. You see, with a chap like me it's a trade; I'm brought up to it an' naturally I become a good workman. My bread an' butter depends on it. Now, with you it's different. You do it by way of a bit of sport, though, mind you, I admit that some o' you chaps play the game uncommon well. You see what I'm drivin' at ? Just think of a man who has to go to sea for a livin' an' one. o' these chaps who go yachtin', then you'll under- stand it better, I woret put it blunter, because I don't wish to hurt your feelin's." "All right," said Mark, bitterly, stung once more by this unexpected taunt. "I mayn't' be the real thing, as more than one of you chaps have been ready to point out; but, all the same, it doesn't alter the fact that since I left home I've lived and fought as hard as any one of you when I've had to do it, and haven't expected to be made 'a colonel for it, either. And as for the rest, I suppose a Martini in the leg's a,s bad for me as if I'd been a regular Tommy, isn't it ? " His companion looked surprised, then bewildered; then lie admitted that there was something in what Mark said, and that, after all, he supposed an Imperial Yeoman was as good as a Lifeguardstnan. "That's something," said Mark. "You'll be admitting that we're soldiers next." "Yes; when you've seen a bit more service and lost a few more men," agreed the soldier, who was of the school which judges of the severity of a fight solely according to the number of killed and wounded at the end of the engagement. "You've told me a lot about the hospital," said Mark, "but' you've said nothing about the nurses. What are they like? How many are there?" "A matter of fifty, all told," said the soldier. "As for what they'rja like, well, they're all good, they're as like melt other as peas in that way. Mind you, they are nurses, .too. No amateurs, about it, neither. They all know their work an' do it; an' I can tell you it's something to 'ave 'ern about the place, seem' that they're all from first-rate hospitals at 'mite, London an' the provinces. But, mind you, I didn't always 'old with. 'em, I mean with women in hospitals. I believed in the good old hospital orderly. Besides, there are so many things that Tommy don't fancy women for when he's laid on his back in the straw. But that , apart, many's the life that's been saved by the nurses at the base, and many's the death that's been easier by 'ern, too. God bless 'em! But was you specially interested in the nurses, or any one of 'REII ? " "There was one," answered Mark. "Ah, I thought as much," said the soldier, with a sigh of pleasure at the clearness of his mental vision. "You don't easily blind me; go on. Though I must say that I thought a few of 'em were sweet on, the doctors. However, at such places, it's bound to be so. Go on, describe her, then I'll tell you if I ever came across her." "Well," said Mark, rather awkwardly, "it's a bit hard. I'm no good at descriptions." "No more am I," said the soldier. "Was she dark ?" he asked, helpfully, quite as willing .to tell as Mark was anxious to bear.

"Rather," said Mark, greatly encouraged; "that is to say, she's more dark than she is light:" "Good teeth, bright eyes, sort of wavy 'air?" - continued the soldier. "Wavy 'air with a gleam of brown in it, as it might be ?" "Exactly," replied Mark, eagerly. ' "There was a lot like that," continued the soldier, discouragingly. "But wait a minute, they weren't all one size. That'll 'elp as. Was she , tall?"

"Medium," answered Beim. "She isn't what you'd call tall, and yet she certainly isn't short. She's betwixt an' between, as we say where I come from." "Not without flesh on 'er bones ?" asked the soldier, still engagingly. "She was pretty plump, but I daresay she's gone off a bit," said Mark. "They all do," said Grant. "Their colour

iziaes, too, so I won't as it she 'ad rosy cheeks. Anyway, if there are rosy cheeks they're made, not horn. if YOU understand "

"But what a waste of time," said Mark. "Why didn't I ask you straight off if you'd come across her by telling her name—Lizzie Jackson? Surely you must have heard of it?" He looked anxiously at his companion. "Nurse Lizzie, Nurse Jackson; Sister Lizzie, Sister Jackson; Miss • Lizzie Jackson," repeated Grant, shaking his head slowly. "No, I can't work the name and the description in with any of 'em. But I say," he added, hastily, noticing Benn's disappointment, "you needn't be down in the mouth about it. You must remember that the Major says she's (there, an' he ought to know. He's been there a lot later than I 'ave. Anyway, you'll very soon know with your own eyes. Well, so long, an' good luck. I only wish I stood in your shoes."

"Legs and all ?" asked Mark, with a smile. "Legs an' all, even with Martini bullets in •em both," said Gra.nt, firmly; and there'avas something so encouraging and confident in what he said that Mark refused to dwell longer on the gloomy picture which his companion's earlier utterances had drawn in his imagination. The Major, whose opinion as to Mark's condition had been fully confirmed by medical opinion, was desirous for many things that the patient should go away. The war had been productive- of strange inci.slents, and there was no certainty that amongst them would not be numbered Mark's sudden and mysterious recovery. In such a case there would be nothing for it but to return the horse ; and with so much sorry cattle about, the officer felt that this would be more than a personal calamity; it would affect the whole battalion and impair its efficiency. Since Mark must go, it was better that he should go soon, and accordingly the Major displayed great zeal in despatching him to the base hospital. Other sick and wounded were being sent—cases for both physician and surgeon. There were officers and men from a hard engagement who were wanting still that skilled surgical treatment which it had not been possible for them to get upon the field; and amongst the wounded, continuing their journey, were those who had been injured long ,before in the earlier days of a siege of a little border town. _ _

In learning of the sufferings of some of these,: Mark almost fait that his own were insignificant. As •he journeyed towards Delfontein, too, he heard of so many marvellous recoveries that he began to hope that, bad as his wound was, he could, with his courage and constitution, pull through without the loss of a limb and disfigurement for life.

It seemed a long, weary, exhausting journey to the base; but Mark bore bravely up against it, the flame of his courage fanned by the expectation of again meeting with Lizzie. With him were a number of other Yeomen, and he was surprised to notice that one of the doctors, who had appeared somewhat impassive, became animated and eager on seeing them. Mark was the first of the Yeomen to be carried in by the hospital orderliss ; immediately after him were other members of the Yeomanry whose branch of the service was apparent. Seeing these, the doctor, addressin someone near him whom Mark could not see, said': "Hullo, here comes a batch of your Imperials. You're in luck's Way. Perhaps one of them's seen him, and can tell you where he is,,, The doctor laughed—a kind, little sympathetic laugh—as he spoke, and stepped aside so that his companion could get in front of him. Mark turned his head curiously to look at the doctor. Instead of seeing him, his eyes met those of Lizzie. _ _ .

' For an instant the two had a wild and almost uncontrollable impulse to rush to each. other's ATM. Instinctively their arms stretched forth to one another; then they remembered that people were watching. "Hullo, Liz," said Mark, sinking back upon the stretcher. "I'm glad to be with you again." He tried to speak as if they had been parted only a day, and as if no perils and privations had been passed through since he saw her last; and he succeeded well enough to Cause his bearers to go on as if nothing had taken place and they had noticed nothing. Lizzie did not utter a word. She turned and looked at her friend the doctor. But her eyes were very full of tears, and the keen Donald knew how perilously near she was to breaking down completely. "That's the way to do it,".said the doctor, heartily, but with something of a mist in his own vision., "It's a thousand times better than making a fuss. Now we've got him, we must make the most of him, eh? I don't think he's very bad. I hope not." "But," whispered Lizzie, almost fiercely, "I don't hope that—l mean—l mean—l hope he's bad enough to be kept here till the war's over. I— I—"

“There, there, that'll do; I understand,” mid the surgeon. "I see it all, really. I'd do the same if I were in your shoes—l would, on my conscience. Besides, I'm certain ,he's had his fair share of it. Lucky Yeoman! I suppose-there are not many who'd change places with him just now, eh?"

The doctor laughed at his own ponderous sarcasm, and then went cheerfully about his business

CHAPTER XX. A WOMAN PREVAILS. _ Faithful and devoted as she had been to all the patients in the hospital since she had entered it, Lizzie had thoughts or care for no one but Mark when he was brought in by the bearers. She had waited long for his coming, and now that he was here she felt that nothing must arise which should part them again. One wish only possessed her, and that was to get him away from a country to which, like so many more, she had come with so light a heart, and from which, like them, she yearned to IV. On every hand she had seen appalling spectacles —the ravages of rifle and artillery fire; the ghastly mischief-making of disease; and time , after time she had prayed that at whatever cost to pride and country the war might end. Only the sternest determination to see her task through to the very end, and the knowledge that she was really doing good, kept her up at all in body, and only the belief and hope that Mark would come through all his dangers safely, sustained her mentally and kept her from crushing, mental depression. Until now she would not have felt that her task was done, or nearly done, and she would have refused to abandon it; but with the return of Mark she could think that the country had no such claim upon her as he himself had. Satisfied on this point, she made it her mission to see first to him and then to join in the ministrations of her comrades to the rest. Donald, the surgeon, watching her, understood. He had seen much of Lizzie and her work, and both she and it appealed to him very powerfully. The little misunderstanding with the lady of title had clinched their companionship, and he was willing to do all he could to help her. In so large a hospital, with so many patients needing treatment, it was not the easiest of things for Lizzie to give to Mark the time and care which she longed to give; but with the help of Donald thil attention was made the easier of fulfilment. At the first moment she was bending over him, anxious to know what he had done and how much he had suffered. In a few brief sentences she learnt all ; as for his wound, her own trained eyes told her that delay had made it serious, and that all the skill and care of the great hospital would be essential to keep Mark from becoming a human wreck.

"I don't know much of doctoring, Liz," he said; "but I've heard some wonderful tales of what your people here have done. Why, it seems nothin-$ nowadays for a man to get a bullet through WO head or body. There's no need then, surely, to worry about a more leg. It's pretty painful, end doesn't look nice; but I suppose you'll soon get me up and about again?" "The doctors are very clever," answered Lizize, with heavy misgivings as to their skill in her lover's ease. "But, you know, they can't do everything." "Why," exclaimed Mark, looking full into her fake, "you don't suppose there's any danger of— of--"

"Hush!" whispered Lizzie. '"There now, take another drink, and don't bother."

"I'd rather die," said Mark, desperately, "than go through life a cripple. What good should Ibo on the farm 'then ? What about riding, getting over hedges and across rough ground; and fifty things that I should have to do when I take the farm over? Tell me that." "You mustn't worry," protested Lizzie. "Now, here comes Dr. Donald. You must see what he RM." -The surgeon, businesslike, collected, came to Mark's bedside and made a swift examination.

- Both Mark and Lizzie watched his face intently; but they gathered nothing from his looks.

===== 0002601_19011213_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Vacc!nation, and how to make the Best of it.

Donalct was not tne man to Detray ens Mounts. Ire was moving quickly away to another bed when Mark respectfully detained linn. "Dare I ask you what you think of it, sir?" lie - said. _ _ _

"Now, no questions," answered the surgeon.. pleasantly. "It'll soon be all right." "If it looks bad, it's because of the terrible knocking about it's had," said Mark, anxious to put the best appearance on his case. "You see, sir, I was starving and tramping on the veld for a whole week. That's upset it a bit." 4 "Upset it a bit!" said Donald. ." The mystery ie that it didn't kill you. You must have the consti- tution of an elephant." "I am pretty tough," said Mark, proudly. "It's a feature of the family. I daresay the thinell look different in a day or two, sir." , He looked anxiously at the doctor as he-spoke. "Yes, it'll look different," answered Donald, moving off. "I say," said Mark, eagerly, bending towards Lizzie, "what's he mean ? There was something queer in the way he said that."

"Oh, nothing, Mark ;don't worry. Just lie down and keep still. You must, you know, or you'll make things worse. There's .nothing to bother about."

"All right, Liz, I'll take your word for it," said Mark, resignedly. "I'm just feeling now as if I could go to sleep for ever—anyway, as if I could go off for a day or two. I suppose I'd better? '

"Yes," said Lizzie, eagerly, smoothing his pillows. "It's the very best thing you could do. Lots of the men who've been brought in have been made new creatures by a long, sound sleep." "Right you are," said Mark. "in the ordinary course of• things I'd keep awake, no matter how hard it was, because I should always fancy • that they'd rush down on me and cart me off and maul me about before I knew what had happened; but now that you're about I'm different. I know you'll see that there's no hanky-panky work." Mark turned his head away, and so intense was his desire for rest that he fell asleep almost instantly. Very rapidly and silently did Lizzie get through her work just then. As soon as it was finished she sought out Donald. He was talking with another member of the medical staff who, Lizzie thought, looked at her with special interest as he left Donald at her approach. "Well ? " said the surgeon. "Your young man is inquisitive. It would be a bit awkward if all patients were like him. In any other case it would have been over by now, You understand what I mean?"

"Yes," answered Lizzie; "but surely it isn't necessary? "Absolutely; there's no doubt on the point," said Donald. "It's the only way. We've arranged to get it done at once. I'm very sorry, but, you know, it can't be helped. I wish you hadn't been about just now. However, I know how sensible you are, and that you'll look at it in the right light."

i‘Butl-but—" said Lizzie, almost helplessly, "are yo certain—quite certain?" "It MT t just my opinion," said Donald. "Ife's been seen by several of us, you know." "But I'm sure you needn't go so far as that," urged Lizzie. "Think what a constitution he has, and what a good rest will do, what it has done so often."

"It's the only thing we can do," persisted Donald. "You've seen enough by this time to know it, too. My dear girl, don't worry, there have been thousands of worse cases since the war began. After all, a man can get along through life as well on one leg as on two. I tell you again, to a man with a constitution like his the thing's a mere detail, if we'd only had the handling of him at the beginning this mightn't have been necessary." "But if it wasn't necessary at first, why now?" asked Lizzie, imploringly. "My dear girl," answered the surgeon, "the answer is so obvious that I won't give it. You might as reasonably ask why it's harder to put a fire out when it's got a firm hold of a building than it is to squelch it when there's only a spark." "Xhen there's no hope? " she asked. "Of saving it? No. I'm very sorry, but I'm sure there isn't. Now, do go and make yourself fit to look after him when he comes out."

"Dr. Donald," she said, laying a hand on hi arm, "I'm so absolutely certain that it needn't bet done that I'm going to ask you to do me a favour, put it off for a day—two days, three, as long as yoc. can ; let's see what we can do. Then if it's- - that we can do 'nothing, Itfon't say an word."

"Very well," answered Donald. "I'll promise, but with a very bad grace. I'll wash my bands of the responsibility. You can take my word for it— you can wait a day or two, or seven—but you're simply putting off the evil for just the length of time you wait."

WARRINGTON WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. Mr. A. H. GrosFie—l—d—on the Need of Social Reforms.

The unfavourable weather on Mondmy last seriously affected the attendance at the annual meeting of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association. Teem was only a small gathering at the tea, which was provided in the Committee Room at seven o'clock, and the subse- quent proceedings in the library were also meagrely attended. Mr A H Crosfield presided, and amongst the ladies present were Mrs Z Oda Armitage (president), Mrs H Roberts (hon. treasurer), Mrs C Jackson Holmes (boa. secretary), Mrs Walker, Mrs Clay, Mrs ain, Mrs Moon- field and others.

ADDRESS BY MISS WIIBBE.

The proceedings eammeacea with an address by Miss Wubbe, of Liverpool, wuo had to leave eatly to catch a train. Tue tholes, she said were very troublous, and It behoved them to tread very carefudy for fear of treading on somebody's corns and do harm where they might do good. A considerable amount of me diffi- culty seemed to have been removed in consequence of the meeting of the Liberal Federation at Denis- a few days ago. She urged them in the words of Longfellow not to " look mournfuily into the past," but to " wisely improve the present and to go forward to meet the future with confidence." That was just what the Liberal Federation did. It said at once ‘• The past is a matter upon which we all hold different views,atici if we hp Ito reunite we will for the presentfput the oast aside. We .an only take the present and the tuture." But was the present a state In which they wished to remain ? Was there anything they wanted to ianprove ? They wanted to get rid at the Conservative Government and to put in their places men the nation could trust and in whom they could have confidence. Tee retrospect was exceedingly gloomy, and instead of having a strong Government they had a family clique, or two family parties who seemed to get on comfortably together—one side by getting his own way, end the otner side by giving in. (Laughter.) In the House of Commons many innovations had been introduced which would have a serious effect upon future legislation if efforts were not made to cheek them. Wubbe referred to the manner in which the financial proposals of tie Government were carried in the last session. The Government wanted to introduce ten minute speeches so that they could closure members and if the English people did not wake up they would ' find their liberties sl.pping away from them. It should be a recognizel thing in the country that paople should 'be able to hold meetings without having them broken. IThere was a sort of reign of disorder, and meetings were not allowed to be held if contrary t popular fas'aion. Let everyone have free speech ; they used t 3 be proud of it. (Hear, hear.) Miss Wubbe proceeded to contrast the condition of South Africa prior to the accession of the present Government and to its condition now. Whatever they might think of the conduct of the war she thought they were agreed it had lasted long enough. We had exacted sufficient punishment for any crimes the Boers might have committed. It was always the part of the stronger power to offer terms and the only terms we had yet offered were unconditional surrender and no shred of independence. These were terms which no free-spirited people could accept. It was for tie Liberal party to offer new terms. It had been sug- gested that it would be necessary to recall Sir Alfred !lifter. His conduct was differently viewed by differ- ent people. It did not seem a generous thing to dis- grace a man when be had been doing his best. None of them thought it WAS a generous manner to treat General Buller —(applause)—as he was treated, and per- haps they would think it was a little bit mean to recall Sir Alfred Milner. But it might became necessary to have a commissioner like Lord Cromer or Lord Rosebery who would command the sympathies and confidence of bothisides. If such a man could be found to go out comosissione Ito bring about terms of peace it would greatly conduce to such terms being found. Reference was made to the efforts of Sir DenryoCampbell Bannerman to unite the Liberal party moue common policy with reference to the war, and Miss Wubbe proceeded to refer to the concentra- tioncampe. She expressed the pleasure that the medi- cal papers were showing the Government what steps wore nee, scary in order to diminish the appalling death rate in the concentration camps. The fiaancial con- dition of the country was also spoken of, Miss Wubbe contending that the country could not coatinue to go on spending 11 millions per week on the war without bringing about: a financial crisis which would seriously affect the trade of the country. The recent speech of the Prince of Wales at the Guildhall was highly commended, and the speaker stated that New Zealand was a type ef what an English colony should be. There they had old age pensions, and the women had Women's Suffrage, two much needed reforms in this moat ry. In conclusion, Miss Wubbe appealed to them to put their heads, hearts and hands together to make the Liberal party what it was in the days of their grand o'd leader. (Applause.) SPEECH BY MR. A. EL CROSFIELD. The CHAIRMAN, who had a cordial reception, said : I wish in the first place to thank you for your kindness in inviting me to join yell this evening, not the first debt of gratitude which in common with other Liberate in our town I owe to the Liberal ladies of Warrington. As gratitude is, we know, a sense of further:favours to come—(hsughter)—l hope we may profit still further by your kindness and support in the future. (Hear, hear). Miss Wubbe in her comprehensive address. whica covered a good deal of ground, naturally alluded to the topic which unfortunately in public affairs still over- shadows everything else. Daring the past twelve months I have done my best to investigate the causes— and the alleged causes—of this disastrous struggle in South Africa and I era afraid you have been treated to rather a long-winded discusq on of that matter in our local press. Well, I take it most of us are agreed upon the following points. In tho first place when Sir Edward Grey tells us that Mr Chamberlain's diplomacy previous to the war was in fact not diplomacy at all, we bold that that admission is in itself a condemnation of the utter folly—if we were to consider its conse- quences we might well say the iniquity—of this new diplomacy. (Applause). We cannot forget the reckless way in which Mr Ceamberlain raised excitement to fever heat by publishing in August 1893, Lord Miler's memorable despatch, practically asking for extreme measures, neither can we forgit that at that critical time he Was publicly ceinparing the President of the Transvaal to a "squeezed sponge,"—one of those amenities which other nations have been less accus- tomed. than ourselves to expect from Birmmgharn— (laughter) —or that, to cap all, towards the close of ' these negotiations the Colonial Secretary must go and raise his terms in the bargain and so break negotiations off. Those are the first grounds on which most of us have condemned the Government's policy, or rather I should say, lack of any sane policy at all. We main- tain—and in this I think I speak for most of us—that no real case or necessity for fighting was ever made out and that with a wise and statesmanlike conduct of efairs we should never have brought the Boers to issuing their ultimatum. (Hear, hear). THE GOVERNMENT'S C)NDOC T OF THE WAR.

In the second plnce, as we did get to lighting and as we are all agreed that the invaders had to be driven from our territory, the first thing to do was to assert our power in the field: I feel very sure there are no two opinions about that in Warrington. (Hear. hear.) But then when we bad done so, after the capture of Bloemfontein, or at any rate alter Lord Roberts reached Pretoria, why did we not make terms with the Boere. Instead of that we treat them to a senseless and impos- sible demand for unconditional surrender, as groat a blunder to my'rnind as the war itself. (Hear, hear.) I heard Sir Edward Grey argue the other day at the Philharmonic in Liverpool that instead of criticising this question of policy we should attack the Government for its inefficient management. I do not agree at all. Their rmanagement or mismanagement of affairs, their lack of knowledge and ordinary foresight has been bad enough, so that Lord Rosebery may well exclaim, " Oh, my Heavens, is the Ananias yet born who will defend them ?" (Laughter): lint important as these details of management are, in politics as in business, the same rule applies, and though close attention to detail is important and indeed absolutely essential, sound policy is even more so still. It has obviously been a terrible mistake to crowd these unfortunate people together in concentration camps ; te send their women and children into crowded camps where the ground was already saturated with the germs of disease. The frightful mortality we see in these camps is as great as ever, and the sooner they are Ibroken up :the better. (Applause). ft-lay's paper seems to give one some hope of this being done,hut in

V7RELT IS TO BE DONE.

And now what is the present position and whit is to be done. I think you will all agree with Me resolution passed at Darby that a Peace Commissioner should be sent out to endeavour to make terms. It is not certain that tie Boers would accept terms based on their incor- poration in the Empire. We have exasperated and Uri von many of them to desperation—(hear. hear),—hat at least the attempt m'ght be made. Fighting to a finish is an ugly enouga prospect for Us, but for the enemy it is a terrible prospect and they know it from experience, Then why should we not semi out some diplomatist of the highest standing as a Peace Coln- minioner ; some man who will not in the eyes of the B oers be responsible for this misery and death among their women and children. EDIICATIDN AND TEMPRRANCE.

I suppose we all agree that no ref rin is more urgent awl pressing than that which shall put our educational system on a reasonable and intelligible footing. That fact is being brought home to us in a very direct way in Warrington at the present moment—(cheers)—but bow are we going to get the thing done. Is there any- body who thinks this effete, playediout government— (laughter)—capable of dealin; with such a question ? Then again, I daresay that on the lines of Lord Peel's report, temperance reform—(hear, bear)—is a thing which we Liberals in Warrington. and particularly the Liberal ladies of Warrington consider to be of para- mount importance. Well does anyone expect we shall get Ibis from the Tories ? Not a chance of it—(hear bear)—and when you bear plenty of people uttering pious opinions in favour of a", reduction of the number of licensed premises—a reduction vitich would clear out the worst and leave the best—l say why doa't they help us ?

===== 0002601_19011213_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOUSING OF THE POOR..

Tae housing of the poor, and toe taxation of ground rents and sites values are two questions which are inter- dependent. Taxation of ground rents and sites values, it may be a dry sound, but what does it mean ? It mmns. that in the first instance I sad which at present is kept unoccupied will be brought into the market. It means neither spoliation nor injustice but it does mean that in the neighbourhood of towns, laud will be obtainable at a reasonable figure instead of fancy prices being demanded for it. I must not take up your time by d &cussing this matter too much in detail but the results of this reform when we carry it, as we have got to carry it—(hear, hear)—will be less pressure of the population on the centres of our crowded cities, cheap tramways to the outskirts, a onance for workingmen to get back and to from their work in reasonable time, cottages with gardens on the outskirts of towns like Warrington where people will ,tae more of the air of heaven to breathe and their children some place better than the gutter to play in. To my mind this reform is so just and so necessary that not only Liberals but Con- servatives of high standing, men like Lord Derby and Lord Stanley of fine public whit must in their hearts approve it. But in the great reforms I have mentioned to you you must not expect the initiative to come from our Conservative friends. No, we have got to provide the initiative and lead the way. We already have a a large backing in Warrington but why should it not be larger still ? Why don't more people—and more 1 ladies.—(hear, hear)—support us ? Well I must not ; take up your time by answering that question at any great length but I will give you a few reasons which no el:Alb"; must of us have heard given in reply to it.

THE ALLEGATIONS AGAINST LIBERALISM. First of all it is allsged—and this allegation is dili- gently circulated on the T3ry side—that Liberalism is antagonistic to discipline. It is nothing of the sort. Liberty is not license. The discipline of the martinet that grudges a man his individuality and seeks to crush out that individuality which has done more than any- thi ag else to make England what she he—(bear, hear,)— that I dislike and repudiate. But it is just as absurd to pretend that our great factories and commercial under- takings can succeed without the employers are masters in their works, as to think you can run a cricket eleven or sail a ship unless the captain has his due authority. Then lately a great dal has been made out of this cry of "Little Englander." With a few unimportant excep- tions there is absolutely nothing in it, and I cannot do better than read you what Sir Henry Campbell Banner- man says on the matter, "They talk of some people being Little Englanders, but I never met a Little Eaglander in the flesh. I have read of him in the news- papers. (Laughter.) It is a phrase invented by some clever newspaper writer, no doubt, and now used as a taunt to be thrown by any worshipper of force and vulgar display which he mistakes for patriotistraagainst I men who are better men than himself." Ia the same speech he says also, " We are all of us proud of the posh- Una of our country in the world, and of our great I E npire. We are anxious to knit in the bonds of; sympathy and confluence all those great communities that have gone out from these shores. Wo are ready to defend our imperial interests, and to add to the Empire if it is necessary." As I have mentioned Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman, and as I am sure you all admire the pluck and devotion to duty with which he is carry- ing out the difficult and rather thankless task in leading the Opposition in the House of Commons at this very difficult titre, may I say that when I met Sir Henry II his house in London the other day I ventured to assure him in your name and in the name of other Liberals in IVarrington that he might depend upon receiving your eor,lial and thorough going supp wt. (Cheers).

===== 0002601_19011213_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
IIiPERIALISM.

But to return to Imperialism, wnat they are striving to do on the :other side is to make support of the Government's infatuated policy in South Africa a con- dition for a man calling himself a sane Imperialist. I absolutely refuse to admit anything of tits kind. (Rear, hear.) The great bulk of the nation, thank goodness, are not Jingoes but they are sane enough Imperialists an i I- agree entirely with Sir Edward Grey that tha Imperialism is not at all likely in the future to be any dividing line between parties. Btfore I sit down I will ' just give you one ether reason, which perhaps More than anything else brings grist to the Tory mill now as ,it always has done. There have always been so many ' worthy but extremely shortsighted and even politically blind people—(laughter)—who regard every reform carried in the interests of tie people at large merely as a stepping stone to wildly revolutionary schemes to follow—schemes that would make for anarchy—sr a's any rate tend in the direction of a vulgarised form of democracy. That is why I think so many people are proud of what they call, " puttieg a dreg on the wheels of the Coach of State." The whoie history of the last century of our politics ptoves they are wrong. IVe can see plain enough that the greater the well being of the people at large, the greater is the stability of our in - stitutions and society, so that as Mr Gladttane himself once put it, "sound but progressive Liberalism is in the end the best form of Conservatism." If, in John Bright's simple phrase "We still have to plead for just laws justly administered," we thereby do not help to destroy but to preserve all that is worth preserving in our country and its institutions. We need not quarrel with our Conservative friends ; some people must of course voice and give'effe.ct to the Conservative instincts whi-211 it is the function of Liberalism to create. But while there are always plenty of people ready for this reason to join what I once heard John Bright call the " EtAndstill Club " and so increase by political inertia and apathy and short-sighted laissez faire the dead weight of the status quo, you ladies fulfil a perhaps mor difficult but far more honourable part in our politics, ' because to you it falls, in Mr. 2,lorley'a phrase, "to help ' to sweii that great tide of progressive opinion upon the force and set of whose currents so largely depends the prosperous voyaging of humanity." (Applause). --e.- A PRISONER FOR SEVENTEEN

YEARS.

An interesting event has just been rennrted by a " Liverpool Courier " representative. For 17 years Mra Elizabeth McNulty, of 49, Price-street, Birkenhead, has been a martyr to a disease which caused her to be prac- tically cut off from the outside world. Pronounced incurable, this poor derelict of humanity abandoned all hope, and re3iened herself to the worst. But a kindly Providence smiled at lase upon her and brought Chas. Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness to her notice. She tried them, with the result that, in spite of the gloomy view of her case by eminent medical men. and opera- tions undergone to no purpose, Mrs McNulty is now perfectly cured, and is daily receitring the congratula- tions of a dumb-founded neighbourhood. This is the wonderful story as detailed by her to a " Courier " reporter : " I had enjoyed as good health as anyone could desire until about seventeen years ago, when after attending upon a neighbour's little girl who was down with scarlet fever I contracted the disease myself. In a matter of seven weeks I recovered, but remained for several msaths in so weak a state that I could scarcely walk across the fl ear. I was racked with fearful pains in the head : so bad were they at times that I could hardly see. After nine months' suffering I saw a doctor,but he did not effect any good. I then lost my appetite, and anything I ate seemed to make my head worse. "At last I became so bad that I could eat no solid food, I had, to drink new Milk to keep myself alive ; and even taking this gave toe a pain between my shoulders which was for all the world like a knife scrap- ing the bone. " Then a new devel minent appeared. A fearful rash broke out all over my face. I took every kind of medi- cine to clear my blood but they only bad the effect of making me worse. What with the agony I was suffer- ing, the other comp'aints, and then this dreadful rash. my cup of bitterness Was indeed full to overflowing ! The rash on m 5 face was so bad that I was ashamed to venture out of my house. It kept me a prisoner within doors.

"After trying more medicines and visiting several doctors. I went to the Skin Hospital in Liverpool. There I was examined by many doctors, and underwent a painful operation; but when tley told me at length that I should have to have my nose scraped, I was frightened, and I never went again. "After a number of years of this terrible suffering. I commenced to have a succession of week-end bilious attack a with remarkable regularity. I can safely say that for upwards of two years,' never knew what it was to be without a bilious attack on Sunday, whim pros- trated me for the whole dar. "One day (it was a Wednesday), on coming down stairs, I founo a little book uoder the door, telling of the wonderf al cures which had been effected by Charles Forde'a Bile Beans. I procured a box, and took one of the beans before I went to bed that night. The next morning my head did not feel so heavy, so I continued taking the Beans, and began to feel better and strongree every day. One Sunday when it Cams to be my usual time to be prostrated with biliousness, I got tbe dinner ready and sat down and ate a hearty meal, without the least sign of any illness ! " My husband said, •, Whatever is the matter with you to-lay ? Are you hot going to be ill ?" Then I tela him how I had taken the B le Beams, and to scion as be saw that they had had such a wonderful effect on me, ,he sent out for another box. By degrees the rash and I spots began to disappear from my face. until I had as clear a complexion as anyone could desire. Above all, I became blessed with a splendid appetite. " Peosle cannot realise that such a wonderful change has been wrought in me so gaickly; and all my neigh- bours, who knew how I suffered, say it is the most miraculous thing they have ever seen. "I feel now as though I don't know my own strength and am as well and hearty as I was when I wag a girl of fourteen. Well, that."—pointing to a huge basket full of clothes which she had just washed,—" is evi- dence that I am strong enough. For the last four months I have had from seven to ten dozen clothes, and sometimes more, every week. and I have washed them all myself. What is more I have felt nothing worse at the end of the day than a natural tired feeling, which a night's sound sleep has rem oved. There is nothing in which I have greater faith than Bile Baana, and I am sure, now that you have heard my story, you will readily understand that."

GREAT SANKEY.

POTATO DISEASES.—A. TeeTi.n.; on "Fungoid Diseases, especially the potato disease " was given in the Hoard Schools, Great Sankey, on Tuesday evening by Mr F P Walker, in conaection vri..h the Winwiek Rural District Committee of the Lancashire County Council (of which Mr T A Murray is chairman) mid the Great S3nkey centre (of which Mr J Fulton Smith is the representa- tive). There was only a small attendance, over which Mr J Fulton Smith presided. —The Chairman, in the course of a brief adclress,said he had to introduce Mr Walker to deliver a lecture on a subject which was of very great importance, and whether they were culti- vators of the potato or consumers, it was interesting to know when they had got a good potato when it was put on the table.—Mr Walker then proceeded with his lecture, which was illustrated with lantern slider. He pointed out that. they did not know when the potato disease first made its appearance, but it was caused by a peculiar kind of fungus, and it was important to remember that the fungus grew not only on the potato itself but upon these plants which were very closely allied to it, such, for instance, as the tomato. The disease, he pointed out, began in the leaves and ulti- mately got to the tubers. The disease spread with great rapidity owing to the fact that the spores of the disease were blown about by the wind. It was thought the disease was produced in a night or two, but as a matter of fact the disease had been in the leaves of the plant or stems some time and had been waiting for the time when it could properly develops.. The lungus was active during July, August and September in' speading. He showed how the spread of the disease should be pre- vented, and gave some valuable advice to the farmers present.

NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington are on the down grade and will soon, I fear, have to be considered an unsuccessful team. Just now they are losing 'matches with painful frequency and occupy a position in the Northern League only fifth from, the bottom. Of course the recent defeats have all been away from home, so that there should be a better time coming when Warrington get a preponder- ance of matches on their own soil. Still they might have done rather better. On Saturday they cut up very poorly ia their game with Batley at Mount Pleas- ant and were soundly beaten at the close by ten points to nil.

Even if Warrington lose Ido like them to make a good show when away from home. There is some con- solation if you leave a good reputation behind, as War- rington did at Broughton and Halifax. But I fear the Batley people will wonder how Warrington managed to defeat the "gallant youths" so easily at Wilderspool pretty much as they wondered at Leeds on the fatal day how Warrington managed to get into the final. They certainly gave few indications of what they really could do in the way of attack, though the defence was pretty formidable, and to a great extent the game was a reproduction of the final for the Northern Union Cup. There was a really striking similarity in the two games.

Batley- put about the best team, in the field that they have had this season, " Watty " Davies turning out for the first time since his suspension after the Warrington match in September. His presence means just about as much to Batley as does Fish to Warrington, and on Saturday it meant considerably more, for Davies led to all his side's points being scored, while Fish was almost idle. Davies and Fish are, I think, without doubt the two finest wing-players under the Northern Union. Both are speedy,clever, and infinitely resourceful. To have them both on one side would be a treat, except for their opponents.

The weather on Saturday was none too pleasant any- where, but at the hilly town of Batley it was decidedly boisterous. Mount Pleasant is the first thing you see on approaching the town by rail, the flag of the Batley Club being visible a long way off, floating bravely in the breeze at an uncommon elevation. Yorkshire folk seem to have a fancy for playing football as near the heavens as they can, which may be estimable enough in some respects, but it is deuced inconvenient. The climb up to Batley's football ground is like scalinz a miniature Spion Kop. In fact there was more than one semblance to Spion Kop about it, for the battle fought on top was decidedly bloody. Once on top and yoi get the full force of the wind, and on Saturday it was very considerable, approaching almost to a gale at the com- mencement of the match.

Warrington were fortunate enough to win the toss— though I would rather they had lost it—and Isherwood naturally took the advantage given him by the breeze. Batley were soon defending, but they showed them- selves capable of something more then defence. Davies put in a tricky run very early on and punting past Hallam gave Oakland an easy try under the posts. The inimitable " Watty kicked a remarkable goal right in the teeth of the gale. Warrington buckled too with a will and play became more than a trifle keen. Dam- aged noses were to be seen on more than one face, and the game looked like devolving into a sanguinary affair. The tackling was of the pile-driving type—picking a man up and then plugging him dawn head-first if he was not too heavy. Some how or other play never seemed to properly open out and Warrington could not get into their stride one bit. The backs were smothered by the alert Batley forwards, and only once did a try look possible, and then Harris failed to pass Garner. A couple of penalty kicks also went wide. Fish was never given a pass, and Burgess was carried off appar- ently badly hurt. At half tirnt, Batley held a lead of 5 points to nil.

Warrington's chance looked very small once they changed ends, even though Burgess turned out again his alleged-to-be-broken collar bone notwithstanding. Batley utilised the wind and kept Warrington defend- ing, and for a long time the defence stood the strain. At last, however, Davies ran down and centred, his forwards taking the ball over the line in a body,though it is questionable whether they were on-side or not. Carroll scored and Davies kicked another goal. Just before the close, Stubley walked off with a broken rib, the disadvantages of playing with which he had ignored until his side were well aliftd.

In most respects the teams were well matched, bit Batley had a long pull in the cleverness Of their for- wards when the ball came loose. These men spread out just as they did it Leeds when the other side got possession, the result being that the Warrington halves and centres were nearly always bottled up. A speedy half back might possibly have broken through but it is questionable: Anyhow they took all the sting out of the Warrington back divi- sion while our men were not nearly so quick on die home backs. I thought the Warrington forwards might have played the man with rather more success than they did seeing the object lesson they were get- ting. They-worked hard in the packs and quite held their own there, but the result was not satisfactlry. The Batley eight were much the more formidable when on the move.

Even the Batley backs showed up the best, and on the day's play were the superior set. NYarrington never really got going, as the fact that Fish did not get a pass from his threequarters abund- antly proves. The halves were mostly to blame, as they kept playing on Harris's wing for some reason, Fish being idle throughout, He never got a chance of showing the Batley crowd any of the qualities which have made him famous in Yorkshire. Burgess was the best of the halves, though there is not a great deal to choose between them. Hallam also did good work, while in the front rank Boardman, Morrison, and Ash- ton were always prominent. Davies was • the star of the Batley side, Auty and Oakland coming in as good , seconds. Spurr, Carroll and Stubley were the best of a dashing set of forwards.

For once in a way the Yorkshire clubs had the best of Saturday's games, only one succumbing to the prowess of a Lancashire team. That one was Bradford who were clearly outplayed at Broughton. Salford ob- tained a single point of Halifax, and that was the sum total of profit gained by the Lancashire clubs over their neighbours.

There were several surprises, one of the biggest being the success of Leigh at Oldham, where goal-kicking decided the contest and Dunbavin was pre-eminent. Runcorn came an awful cropper at Huddersfield, where a defeat of 20 points to 5 points made the Cheshire lads look very small beer. However the score does not in- dicate the play two or three tries being of the lucky order. Still it was a big performance for the Fartowners. Swinton went down before Hull, which was not unex- pected and lVarrington were beaten by Batley—but I have mentioned that before.

Bold-street Wesley Guild.

At the meeting of this Guild on Monday night, Mr George Littler conducted a "Speaking Bee." Some discussion took place at the beginning of the meeting as to what should be the time limit allowed to the speakers, and it was filially decided that they must not exceed two minutes. " Love in a cottage," which was the subject selected, was discussed by Miss Prince and Messrs Bean, McMasters, Wright, T Prince, F L'glat, 8 F Nelsen, and R H Sawa. As the speakers are all unmarried the opinions given were entirely theoretical, but the chief arguments used by the majnity went to prove that the whole question depends upon the style both of the love and of the cottage. All the speeches were of a decidedly humorous character.

Halton Road, (Runcorn), Foreign Missionary Sermons.

The Rev J Kirby, Walkden, Manchester, was the preacher at Halton-road on Sunday, it being the occa- sion of the annual foreign missions. There were good congregations at the morning and evening services and the preacher's sermons, containing interesting accounts of his ten years' services in the West Indies,were listened to with marked appreciation. Toe children's service in the afternoon was a very interesting one, Mr J R Frith occupied the chair and after a most appropriate ad- dress Mr E Taylor read the general report.. Mr J Nall, secretary, gave a full and detailed account of the mis- sionary work of the S.unday school which was highly applauded. Solos were given by Mr G Parr and Mr J Brown. A most successful meeting was closed by Rev J B Morrison pronouncing the Benediction. At the evening service the choir rendered in fine style," The Lord gave the Word."

Open Meeting of Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The meeting of the Wycliffe P.S. A. Society on Sun- day afternoon was an open one, and there was a large attendance of both sexes. Mr F W Bineßoss presided and in referring to the blessings which we enjoyed to- day, spoke of the valuable libraries of books which were placed within the range 01 everyone. In these libraries they had the accumulated wisdom of several thousand years, and yet there were many people who never looked at the gems of literature from which they could derive so much pleasure and profit. The Rev PG Wteq, of Liverpool, was the sneaker, and he took ao his subject, " The painted face." Be refereed to the incident recorded in ii. Kings, 9 chapter, and dwelt more particularly on the 30:h verse "And when Jahu was come to Jezreel, Jezeber heard of it ; and she painted her face, anct tired her bead, and looked out at a window." Jezebel, remarked Mr West, was supposed to be one of the worst characters in literature. It was atronishlng„ he said, what some women could do, either for good or ill. Yet some of the mest beautiful charac- ters in the world were those of women. There was a great deal of paint in tke world now. They were paint- ing their own characters, and the face was the place where the colours showed. It was astonishing how their character came out in the face ; but nevertheless a man might have a handsome lace and a b ,c 1 character. Formality would not hide the sins, or stay the judgment of God. They should not be satisfied with outside form, ceremony and appearance. He trusted that as a result of attending the P.S.A. meetings they were living better lives. Two solos were very efiicientiy sung by Mr Levi Richardson. Mr WII Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ. The Rev John Tongs offered prayer and an interesting meeting 'closed with the pronouncing of the B mediction. A collection was taken on belealt of the society 's funds.

Sermon to Young Men.

Goudie as a Warning.

At Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening, the Bev John Yonge preached a special sermon to young mem Toe preacher based h's di.course on the text "Toe man that wandereth out of the way of understanding shall remain in the congregation of the dead." Proverbs 21 ch., 16v. He remarked that these were words of admomtion. intended to warn them against the danger of diverging and departing from the clearly defined way of under- standing, because of the inevitable consequences. The subject called them to consider what the way of under- standing was ; how toes' might wander from it, and what would become of the wandering. When young men broke loose from religious assoalations, and took up from choice the associations and habits and ideas of the wry opposite, they were sadly wandering. The preacher referred to the recent example afforded them br the glaring deed of Gaudie, the Liv?rpool bank clerk. Toat yonng man diverted from the path of rectitude when he committed the first fraud and no onm•ever discovered it. So elated by the success of his first fraudulent act he wandered further and furthn out of the way at understanding until the hour struck when discovery stared him in the face and be turned and:fled. Then in that awful moment did

they not think that that young man leaned that he was not only &criminal but one of the biggest fools on the face of Gan earth. What did they fancy was the nature of his reflections as he awaited his trial in London. He might be planning some line of defence ; he might be hoping for some lucky thing to turn up that would mitigate a portion of his penalty ; he might be hoping years hence, to have another chance in Fo rn distant part of the world ; but his bitterest feeling,d own deepest in his heart, would be the unutterable tally of it all. The preacher concluded with an elovent appeal to the you e.g men to resist the beginning oi wandering from the right path.

WARRINGTON.

Humorous Carnival at Parr Hall.

The first of two humorous carnivals promoted by Messrs Kilduff and Hardimani was held in the Parr Hall on Monday evening. A capital pro- gramme had been arranged and a number of well- known artistes appeared. The attendance of the public was small. The principal items ef the even- ing were the mandoline solos by Mdlle. Marienne Tostia, whose remarkably skilful handling of the instrument elicited the cordial approbation of the audience. Mddle. Elso Tostia gave two pianoforte silos in brilliant. style and her contributions were highly appreciated. Other interesting featares of a most attractive programme were the musical and mimetical entertainments by the Bros. Benson, humorous selections by Mr Matt Kilduff and Mr Mark Tyme, lightning cartoons of celebrated men and smoke and scene painting by Mr Penton Cross, and recitations by Mr W Kilduff. The last named gentleman displayed great elocu- tionary ability, his recitations being " One winter's night" and "The uncle." Mr A Holt, at Manches- ter, was an efficient accompanist.

Complimentary to Warrington. An elderly man with grey head and beard, whose name is William Thomas Smith, was brought up at the Manchester City Police-court on Saturday and cinfronted by two women he had married—thereat Mrs Smith in 1870, and Elizabeth Kellv, 18,Richard- son-street, Rochdale-road, on June 29,1885. We believe you to be a great scoundrel.—Prisoner : That has to be proved. —As the prisoner was being removed there was a commotion at the hack of the court, and a police constable was beard reminding someone the, " You ,are not at Warrington now."
