FOOD FOR THE LIONS. Menagerie Proprietor Summoned at Warrington.

Interesting Case.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, before Mr, F. Monks and other magistrates, William Sedge- wick, menagerie proprietor, was summoned by the R.S.P.C.A. for illtreating a horse by travelling it while in an unfit condition on November 21st. Stephen Rose, an employe, was summoned for illtrearing two horses by travelting them while in an ucfit condition on the same date, and Arthur Sedgewick, sou of the first defendant. was summoned for causiog the horses to be travelled.—Mr Browne appeared for the defendante. —Rose did not appear. It was stated that the summons had been served at the house of his parents in Runcorn, and be was travelling with the menagerie. The first witness was Inepector Taylor who said that at 12.30 p.m., on the date mectianed,ahe was at Bridge Foot, aad saw the caravans of the menager:e come ever the bridge and turn into arpley-road. A arey mare, loose and uottarneased, was following the last van. The animal was very larne on the off foie log. He caught it and asked one of the men where the owner was: He replied that he was on the front van. He (witness) had the mare taken to the Eagle sod Coild Hotel yard. He saw Mr Sedgewick and a veterinary surgeon there. Be called Mr Sedgewick's attention to the mare, and be said " I didn't think it was so bad as that." Witness informed Inspector Print of the matter, and, ascertaic- ing that two other horses were coming on the road, went away with the Inspector, and in Bold-street saw the defendant Rose leading a grey gelding. The animal was in a shocking state and was lame all round. Rase said he had left another horse at the Norton Arms stable. Witness and the Inspector took tne gelding to the Eagle and Child Hotel yard, and Mr Sedgewick wanted to have it destroyed. They aiterwards went to the Norton Arms, and saw a grey mare there. It took the animal all its time to get out of the stable. He had never seen such a sight of a horse in all his tile. The near fore foot was completely rotting away, :led the other was spread out like that of a cow. It seemed that the two animals had been bought from a farmer for £3. Mr Sedgewick said be would not have had such a thing happen for 50.—Cross-7xsznined : There were about eighteen caravans, and Mr Sedgewick was in the front The first horse was still in the Eagle and Child Hotel stable,and was being attended by a veterinary surgeon. Mr Sedgewick said the two other animals bad been bought for food for the lions. In reply to a remark frem the Chairman, Mr Browne Said the first horse was prououcced by the veterinary to be curable. _

P.C. Edwards and Inspector Print also gave evidence. Mr. Browne said that Mr. Sedgewick had been on the road with the menagerie eighteen years, and there had never before been a complaint against him. On the date mentioned he had corns from Runcorn and was going through Warrington with eighteen caravans. Be had about 30 horses for the purpose of drawing the caravans, and the vans were so heavy that it was a. necessity they should be good horses. He bad had the first horse for five fears, and it had been a very useful servant to him. A week before the date in question it went a bit lame. He thought it bad a corn, and had thei shoe removed. He found his surmise was not correct, and he had not worked it from that moment. It was allowed to be loose. and merely waited behind the cara- vans. When Mr Sedgewick saw the animal at Warring- ton he saw that the animal had gone much worse, and he said, as Inspector Taylor had given fa, that he did not know it was so bad as that. He called in a veterinary surgeon. Inspector Print wanted to have the animal destroyed. Mr Richardson, the veterinary surgecn. said no ; it was possible to cure it. There was no intentional cruelty here, and that must be proved. The other cases were of a totally diffarent character. They bad to buy horses for fotA for the lions. Mr Arthur Seilgewick was in the last van. His father knew rothing of this matter. Mr Arthur Sedgewick saw a , horse in a field at Daresburr, and thougbt. it would do for the lions. He saw tle farmer, and was shown a second horse and beught the two for £3. He could not kill them on the road. He must take them to the knackers. He told Rose to take them to Tool,i's at Warrington. There was no other Lod for the lions that night, and the bones bad to be taken to Warrington to be destroyed. He strongly submitted that Mr Arthur Sedgewick had committed no offence. Tra.s Chairman said the system was oven to abuse. A man might buy horses under such conditions and work them.

Mr Browns: These were not being worked. Inspector Print said tale horses were going to be travelled to Earlestown, Mr Brawn° said tbat. was not. true. Mr Sedgewick's word was gulte as good Ise the im•pector's. Inapector Print said the horses should have been killed on the farm.

Mr Browne again said the horses were not being taken to Earlestown. There was. he said, no knacke: there. The horses were being taken to Toole's. He also said that the inspector would not have known about the third horse, had he not been openly told that it was in the stable at the Norton Arms.

It was admitted that no one bad seen the last mentioned horse on the road. _ _

The Construction of Reservoirs.

_ The half-yearly meeting of the Association of Waterworks' Engineers NVEL9 held in London on, Saturday, amongst those present being Mr G Mit- chell, of Warrington. A discussion took place on. the design and construction of open and covered service reservoirs. Following a paper read by Mr William Ingham at the Birkenhead meeting, Mr , Mitchell, of Warrington, criticized the puddling of ! walls and lions of reservoirs. Papers were read on the French, English, and American mt•thods of manufacturing cast-iron pipes. Mr Stephen II Terry said that Great Britain had the reputation amongst certain foreign nations of adhering too closely to existing methods in working and of being slow to adopt labour-saving appliances in engineering and kindred trades but although there might be some truth in this, he thought that in pipe-founding, at any rate, we were determined to be in the fore- front, and were no going to permit the intelligent and withal industrious toreigner—be he French German, Cr American—to beat us in cost, produc- tion, or quality.

At the Earlewown Police ISsation on Monday morning, before Mr J W f•ramett, Michel Coyne, John Jones, Edward Harrington, and Robert Clarke, were charged with being :drunk and disorderly its .Earlestown on Saturday night, and were each fined 23 6d and costs.

THE EXAMINER.

DECEMBER 13TH 1901.

It is only fair, when Trade-Union British manufacturers and Vagaries. merchants are so freely criticised for allowing trade with other countries to pass out of their hands to foreign competitors, to consider what share of this unfortunate tend- ency is to be attributed to the British workman and his trade organisations. A carrespondencel on the subject has been going on in the columns of the" Times," and if even a tithe of the charges there made against trades unions be true, a strong ease has been made out against these organisations for deliberately setting back the producing power of the trades which they represent, and which they have Come to a large extent to regulate and dom- inate. It is oaly reasonable to expect that with improved machinery and better methods of organ- isation workmen should be able to turn out better work and more of it; and while this is to some ex- tent true, there is sufficient evidence to show that the workman himself does not keep pace with the • general improvement. Not only so, but it seems as if the lagging were deliberate —systematically carried out in fulfilment of trade regulations, under the fallacious belief that thereby a benefit was being conferred on the workmen. A disposition seems to be shown, now that hours have been shortened and wages increased,to reduce the output to the capacity of the least capable of the workmen. This is done on the plea of making things nice and comfortable all round—no doubt one idea of brotherhood and good-fellowship, but a short-sighted vagary at that. If trades union- ists could take a farther view, they would see that while the exigencies of a trade may bear the strain of short hours and high wages provided plenty of good work is turned out as the result—if, instead, work is slowly and slovenly done, and there is a general levelling down of output, that will inevit- ably prove the last straw that will break the camel's back. On humanitarian grounds all right- thinking citizens will approve of short hours and good wages; but the improved social and moral condition of the workman which these benefits imply call for a return not only in better work but more of it. If this return is not forthcoming, then the economic law is broken, the commer- cial machine is thrown out of gear, and disaster must inevitably follow. We hear of bricklayers doing just half the work they used to do, though trade conditions have very inuah Improved. There are engineers' trades unions which fine a foreman because he has driven in a rivet which his squad have left out when they went on the spree. We are told of printers who will not allow the type-setting machines to be worked faster than a given rate, a complaint which is prevalent in all kinds of machine shops, where the employers complain they do not get value out of their machinery, and are thereby handicapped in competing for new business. Carpenters carry the fast automatic screw-drivers in their pockets; • and use the old-fashioned slow tool for their work; and foremen allow the men to loaf round half of their time on an outside job lest it should be too soon finished. Now„ these vargaries of human depravity are neither moral nor sane, and sooner or later they must bring Nemesis In their track as surely as night follows day." It is already appearing in the diminished returns of our trade at home and abroad, and if severe penalties are to be averted it must only be by trades unions seeing it to be a part of their duty, for which they are morally responsible, that for a -fair wage a fair day's work will be given. There is no reason in common-sense why a strong and capable workman should have to restrict his out- put to the level of weaker men. Quite the reverse and if no other solution of the problem can be found, then a return to piece-work will have to be faced in the interests of trade, which means in the interests of the workers themseves. , e„,

THERE are indications An Antidote to which seem to point to Anglophobia. the strong probability that Anglophobia on the Continent is on the wane. A curious story is reported in a London paper of a French cadet, a medallist of the cavalry school at Saumur, who on Saturday expressed a supreme desire to serve the KING of England in South Africa. If accepted, this valiant French- man declares that his offer will be followed by forty others from the cream of the cavalry regi- ments of France. It is possible that the War Office may regard the offer of service as a mere vagary of the eccentric French mind; though it is matter of history that soldiers of fortune from England and Scotland and Ireland have before now served in the armies of France, under King or Emperor. Whatever be its explanation, the incident shows that there is another side to the "A bits le. Anglais! "which is so often represented as a, national cry. In Germany the press seems to desire nothing more than a cessation of the recent hostile feeling displayed against this country,which it is now coming to be recognised was a mistake from every point ot view. The Vessi3che Zoitung indulges the hope and opinion that the storm which has arisen will cease with the termination of the war, adding, by way of salve to the disappointed agitators, that " It would be a crime for a great nation to sacrifice its interests to aid a small nation which never requested nor followed the advice of Germany." Teat is true and reasonable ; but perhaps the main cause to which the volto face of so many German news- papers may be attributed is to be found in the fact that :a storm, greater, and momentous to Germany, is breaking out in its Polish provinces, rid, indeed, through the whole of that conquered ingdem, Probably the best antidote to A nglo- phobia will be found in this racial trouble which the Continental Powers now find at their own door, as the result of the agitation in favour of Boerism which has been fostered in the press and among the populace. They have indulged in a feverish passion of racial nationalism, and have not scrupled to use distortion and misrepresenta- tion in feeding the flame, Now they are reaping what they have sown. If nationalism is be devel- oped to the point of separation, then neither Russia, nor Austria, nor Germany is safe. Each of these great Powers is a composite State made up of many nationalities, combined on the whole for a common interest—combined in some cases, however, with less justification than Britain has now for seeking to incorporate the Dutch South African Republics in her Empire. Carried away with thn pro-Boer sentiment, the Poles have argued logically enough that what was good for the Boers was good for them,and even more just. How the Continental Powers treat such senti- ment when it comes home to themselves is being well illustrated at Posen, where school-children are being treated as criminals because they insist on being taught religion in their own language, and where a learned doctr and journalist, for writing three articles on the subject, has been sen- tenced to two years' imprisonment. Writers in the German Press have expressed great indignation at the acts of the German army being cited in comparison with those of our soldiers in South Africa. It will be interesting to watch the treat- ment of the malcontent Poles, and to compare it with British treatment of the Boers under cir- cumstances of much greater aggravation. The result will be probably a very considerable revul- sion of Continental feeling in favour of British methods, which even falsehood and slander will be unable to stem or overthrow.

Truly the intricacies of the halfpenny post regula- tions :are wonderful. A few days ago it was practically demonstrated by a correspondent of the " Times " that a letter to the editor, intended for publication, might pass with a halfpenny stamp if only inscribed "Printers' Copy." Had, how- ever, the writer " spatchcocked " into his letter an invitation to Mr Buckle to dine with him next evening, the letter would have been surcharged. For the regulations,therefore to be strictly carried out all communications sent as printers' copy should be read through by some official, whose salary would be very poorly earned by the result. Perhaps, however, an even absurder point is suggested by a Welsh newspaper pro- prietor, who has been Retailing his grievance to the ready ear of the editor of " Truth." It ap- pears that an order for the insertion of an adver- t.semeat was sent to the office in an open envelope bearing a halfpenny stamp, and was surcharged a penny on the ground that it contained a com- munication in the nature of a letter. As a matter of principle an appeal was made to St. Martin's- le-Grand, and it was pointed out that "orders for goods" are among the commercial documents which are enumerated in the Post-Office Guide as transmissible at the book-post rate. To this the Secretary of the Post Oilhe replied gravely in the best official manner that an order for an advertisement does not conform to the definition of an "order for goods." Marvellous the ingenuity which can set up this futile distinction!

Motor-car manufacturers in this country have been gradually catching up with their rivals in France—who, it must be remembered, had a very long start of them ; but it cannot , we fear, yet be said that the competition takes place on equal terms. We do not, it is true, want in this country the powerful racing machines which, with motors ,of twenty horse-power or so, enable French ' chauffeurs to skim along the roads at the rate of an express train. These automobiles, which are splendidly represented in the international show which has just opened in Paris, are wonderful contrivances, testifying amply to the skill and in- genuity of their builders, but we do not necessar- ily wish them on our roads. Their use is a sport for millionaires, or at least for " six-figure " men, and the wealthy classes. here have not, happily, yet caught the crate What is wanted is a light car of reasonable speed, and here again the French model must, it is to be feared, still be pronounced superior. The very type is better. The Continental designs are har- monious and rational; the English still for the most part see the automobile as a carriage without horses,and not as a thing apart from all the horsed traffic that has ever gone on roads, a now form of locomotive to develop entirely in its own way. Whatever the future of the automobile for goods carriages—and the trials by the War Office do not seem to have been altogether satisfactory— we have a strong suspicion that there has yet to be produced in this country a type the appearance, the price, and the reliability of which will really commend it to use by professional men and for the ordinary purpose of pleasure.

PUBLIC BENEFIT UNDER TN& OLOCKIIrOIt ST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt THE GALE AT RUNCORN.

From Sunday until Wednesday a somewhat severe gale swept over Runcorn and district. Hail, sleet, snow, rain and wind, co-mingled made things as unpleasant for the inhabitants as it was possible for them to be. Fortunately no serious damage has yet been reported. There was, of course, the usual number of broken chimney pots and slates lying on some of the footpaths, but they have happily caused no casual- ties. The Mersey, however, forced by the wind, rose several feet higher on Monday and Tuesday, and inconvenienced shipping at the docks gener- ally. The gale may safely be reckoned as the pre- cursor to the real setting in of Christmas weather. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MINERAL ATEES.—Advt SIR JOHN T. BRUNNER'S GENEROSITY.

MATTERS OF MOMENT

A Provincial Letter.

Christmas is coming, and so is Coronation Day Evidence of the former, if not sufficiently apparent as yet in the shop windows, is afforded by the Post Office notice that in order to secure arrival at or a`sout Christmas Day, letters for Canada, and for the United States by way of New York, must be posted in time for the mail which leaves Liverpool to-day (Saturday). Proclamation definitely fixing the date of the Coronation,June 20th, is published, in stately and interesting archaic language, and the announce- menu thus made will have the effect of giving an, additional impetus to a multitude of preparations already being pursued in most parts of the pro- vinces. One effect of the contemplated feastings and rejoicings has been to cause many provincial mayors to have their "salaries raised "—for one year only—and it has yet to be seen, of course, whether those who voted the money with a light heart in these cases, will do ditto ru the event of being asked by their respective employes to be similarly remembered. Another matter which has been placed beyond the boundary line of specula- tion, by Kingly proclamation, is the date fixed for the opening of Parliament, which is the 16th of January. So that we now know fairly well where we are and what to look forward to, as regards some of the most important matters which concern us as " provincials " to the middle of next year, when, let us at least indulge the hips that we may be there to see, and to take part in the national rejoicings over the Coronation of King Edward the Seventh. When a certain number of business men inter- ested in the Telephone Service waited upon Lord Londonderry the other day, as a deputation ap- pointed to obtain better terms, if possible, it was felt that there was not much hope in that quarter. And when the interview was over it came to pass that certain of those who constituted the deputa- tion felt as if they might just as well have remained at home. This is a matter in which the provinces are especially concerned, and on the very day that Lord Londonderry was showing the gentlemen referred to, how impossible it was to compete against the National Telephone Company, the Mayor and Town Clerk of Grantham, in Lincoln- shire, it is reported, were "proving " before an Inspector of the Local Government Board how with a licence from the Post Orfila granted in September last year, they could establish very effective competition in that town. The area of that licence is described as the borough of Grant- ham, with surrounding parishes within a radius of ten to twelve miles, and the Corporation's intention is to give a local service, as well as a trunk line service, at a cost of three pounds. The Company's charges begin at eight and go down to six pounds, but in order to meet the Corporation's dangerous charges, the report says that the Company now make an offer which works out at £3 Os Si per year!

With fighting in three Continents the prospects of kehristmas at peace throughout the world are deciaedly slim. Notwithstanding unmistakable signs of collapse, hostilities in South Africa will certainly be carried forward to the ensuing year. John Bull's forces are also engaged on the north- western frontier of India, and in the West-African hinterland. There are further threats of a rising in a remote district of Nigeria; while in South America the Colombians and the Venezuelans are still engaged in deadly strife, and the Republics of Chili and Argentina threaten to come to blows. The , hard knocks which the belligerents in South America are apt to exchange with one another, do not greatly affect British interests; though the ramifications of John Bull's trade are such that there must necessarily be some minor disorganis- ations. An outbreak of war between the Argentine Republic and Chili, for instance, would have con- sequences so far-reaching as to be felt even in tne English provinces. Vast numbers of cattle, sheep, and horses are exported from the Argentine ports, and not a few of the latter are at this moment in the service of our Army in South Africa. The difficulty for this country would be if the Chilian navy were strong enough to blockade the Argentine ports. Then the grain-laden argosies for Great Britain would be stopped, and the British corn trade—and the provincial consumer in due time— would assuredly have to bear the pinch.

Provincial towns which have been exercised one way or other as to the Lord's Day Observance Act are indebted to the Town Clerk of Bristol for having ascertained on behalf of the Watch Committee in that city in what other cities and towns the pro- visions of that act are strictly enforced, and whether such towns have local Acts dealing with the matter of Sunday trading. From the replies received the Town Clerk prepared a report in which he states that in Bolton, Bradford, Derby, Leeds, Leicester, Liverpool, Manchester. Newcastle, Nor- wich, Nottingham, Oldhaw, and Salford the pro- visions of the Act are not enforced, nor had those olae.es any local powers enabling them to stop Sunday trading. The Corporation of Oldham re- cently obtained statutory power bye-laws prohibit- ing the crying of newspapers in the streets on Sun- daysln Huddersfield the Act is not strictly enforced; that is to say, the police take no action unless special complaints are made. A few years azo attempts were made to enforce the Lord's Day Ob- servance Act, but so much hostile criticism was aroused as to cause the efforts to be abandoned. Tie Act is enforced in Hull. During last year 4,082 persons were proceeded against for breach of the Act and 4,082 coavictions were ob- tained. The Town Clerk of UuU however, adds that the maximum fins of 53 is too small to act as a deterrent Co those who wish to trade on Sundays. At Birmingham, it is set, forth, the Watch Committee some years since instituted proceedings under the Lord's Day Observance Act, but their action excited considerable comment, and since then the Act has become practically a dead letter. In Sheffield action has from time to time been taken by private individuals under the Act,but the Town Clerk of Sheffield says the magistrites have always been very reluctant to convict. It is noteworthy in this connection, that the object of the Act of Charles ll,,and the other and practically obsolete Acts of Charles 1., was to enforce a strict observance of the Sabbath, and not mainly to re- strict trade, and tha,, if these Acts were to be revived and enforced it would be necessary to pro- ceed again Olt many other persons besides small shopkeepers and news-vendors.

At the Conference on the 'lousing of the Poor, it will have been noted by newspaper readers, although held at Westminster the speeches de- livered were almost exclusively directed to the state of things alleged to exist in the provinces. Deplorable pictures were drawn of overcrowded and unhealthy hovels in our rural villages, as well as in some of the larger provincial towns, and a resolution was passed declaring this to be a grow- ing scandal and menance to the well-being of the nation. With all of which it is impossible not to agree. A demand was however made for a furthEr Act to enable local authorities to a^guire, compul- sorily, land for building cottages with gar4ens, and to'extend the periods for repayment of loans for such purposes. But. how about the Acts of Parliament already available? Many things are at present more urgently needed than"cottagers with gardens" desirable as these may be. and it is not easy to find where the requirements of the towns come in. The everlasting demand for new Acts of Parliament i must now be classed n the category of makeshifts for shelving admitted grievances in which the com- mon resort to Royal Commissioners, and " com- mittees of investigation," is included. There does not appear to be anything ireal or sincere about the i demand, except that it s ntended as a further means of marking time.

On the authority of Commissions it is known that even with the constant migration to populous places, over-crowding is a crying evil in many rural districts, and that it leads to a terrible amount of immorality. Still it is questionable whether all the housing schemes that have been projected, and they are many, and all the plans of" philanthrophy at five per cent." would woo the intelligent toiler back to the land unless he were assured that not only his material prospects but his social environment would be brightened; conditions which seem to be either lost sight of entirely by the would-be reformer, or to be in other ways im- possible of fulfilment. It has come to be recog- nised that, as a matter of fact many of our smaller villages have already been" drained of their best types of manhood and womanhood, and that the evils of intermarrying are beginning to tell. So that the weaker portion are stay-at-homes, and the decay of the rural population follows as one of the , inexorable laws of nature.

jaaN WALKER AND CO., B ,A7SEY STREET. WARRINGTON

PRINTIYG OF ALL DESCRIPTIONS

WITH NEATNESS AND DESPATCH.

INVOICES, BUSINESS CARDS, CIRCULARS, MEMORANDUMS,

RUNCORN NOTES. [BY A CORRESPONDENT.I

Many people anticipated that the last meeting of the Scnool Board coming immediately as it dick after such a keen contest would have been an occa- sion for mutual recrimination. There was, of course , nothing of the kind and right thinking people will be glad that there was not. As a matter ot fact there was nothing to fail out about even had the , members wished unless it had been the prize which the ratepayers have to pay for the cost a the elec- tion. No true lover of education, however, will deny that the amount incurred to gain such a Board which we now possess, has peen money well spent. The next meeting of the Board is by far the most important as it will be the first men- ing of the New Board. It will be then decided who ; is to fill the important offices in connection with the Board such as chairman and vice chairman With all due respect to the present voltintaryists who occupy these important pasts it can easily be understood that progressive members must fill these seats of authority. It is, at present, impolitic to go into the .` why" of the question but one thing is certain, that if the Progressives now stay. their hand not only will they jeopardise their present position but alienate the sympathy of a good many of their supporters.

At present, as one of the results of the election, there is quite a little sensation convulsing the town owing to the publication of a challenge by Mr Baden to the Rev H B Firth. Can,Reines, by the way, seem now to be the order of the day. No one appears content to repudiate a statement or make an affirmation unless he introduces the word "challenge," which to the unsophisticated smacks of something eminently warlike. But tha word, in spite of its martial sound, is a very harmless one and means very little. No one need be afraid though it does sound big on paper. One cannot help thinking that this controversy as to "incense" anti" absolution" is one of those side issues which should have closed at the latest, with the result of the poll. Suppose Mr Boden is right, aud is justly " incensed" at the use of " incense" and genuinely wished to "absolve' himself from the effects of "absolution" given five years ago, what, in the name of all that's honest, has this got to do with the education question ? I know the natural answer of the Progressive,who disbelieves in creed taught at ,rday schools, to this question, but it essentially fails to hit the mark. Let the Noncon- formists who clamour so loudly for toleration be more considerate towards those whose tempera- ments are not suited to the plain unvarnished veri- ties of the religion which they believe. And if a man wishes to have the surpliced choir and the ornate ritual why should any one (Zaire to stand in his way. As one writer finely puts it " All paths ; lead to Heaven." True Evangelical and Ritualist ; meet but in one desire and make but one music.

--- At a mee7,lng neld at Brunswick School sorre faw days ago, which was exceptionally well attended, a goodly number of persons during an interval visit- ed the lower school-room, and were surprised to find that it possessed a gymnasium second to none in the town. At present there is an outcry made in many quarters that there are not sufficient places of amuz:ement and healthy recreation for the young men of the town. To a certain extent this is quite correct, and it's a pity 'cis so. But in one branch of culture—that of athleticism—there is a splendid opportunity which may never return to the young men of the town. A spacious gymnasium. fitted with all the modern appliances for the cultivation of bone and muscle, is open to be used by everyone yet strange to say, with the exception of the first season or so, the support for the last three years has been distinctly discouraging to those connect- ed with it. Why this should be so is hard to Pay Like the one associated with the Runcorn Y fC A, it seems that as soon as the charm of novelty has worn off, its value so depreciates as to make it worthless to the majority of the inhabitants. If, however, this institution is allowe I to lapse into the stage of thee' have beens "and the " are nots," not only will it be a lasting dishonour to the youths of the circuit, but also to the inhabitants at large.

Cultivation of the physique is important but the expansion of the intellect is equally necessary. A finely proportioned body and a well-balanced mind should always go together. At present the town really stands in need of a thorough good debating society where the young men of the town and dis- trict can find their oratorical legs. Sal to say a town like Runcorn, boasting such " an enlightened electorate " absolutely cannot pride itself on possessing a first class debating or literary society. The advantages of such an institution are too appar- ent to enumerate. It is pleasing to chronicle that there have been individualistic attempts made to float such an organization, but, more or le4s, each has beenidoomed to ultimate failure. "Tne Pickwick Club" started with a flourish of trumpets whim commanded success for a season but after one or two public meetings enthusiasm dwindled until the members were whittled down to less than half-a-dozen and these had to wind the concern up. This year has seen the death and burial of the Shorthand writers association," an organization not quite so comprehensive but emi- nently deserving of support. And so it would be possible to go on pointing out different classes in connection with various places of worship ,to • that have had to give up the ghost through lack of support. The present time sees the "Bruns- wick Mutual" with a magn•ficent record of forty years standing, which now, unfortunately drags out but a weary existence and :the " Ellesmere-street Debating Society " which is about equally well attended. Now what is to prevent these two societies amalgamating and forming a distinct literary and debating society free from any re- ligious body and quite open—as, indeed, they are at present—to every one in the town. There is no'doubt that the nucleus of a strong society is there and the materials are to hand. There need, perhaps, be nothing so ambitious as a Parliamentary debating society but merely an" open door " society which every young fellow could feel was a training ground for the sterner work of public life in which he might be called to participate in the nest future.

Two men, a boiled ham, and a police sergeant. So runs the tale told at length in another column. Let imagination run riot and see what a fanciful picture is conjured up. A winter's night, the stars twinkle dimly and the flickering gas lamps scarcely pierce the darkness. Church-street h wrapped in silence. The monotonous tramp of the police officer is no longer heard. The city sleeps, Hark I a footfall soundi. There lurking in the shadows are two men crouching and moving slowly towards a small shop through whose window chinks peers a dim light. Apparently they are fascinated by something, for the struggling moon beams disclose a look of eap_er expectancy:in their eyes. :Cautiou,ly the shop door latch is lifted and the two tread noiseles4y inside• A moment later they are outside holding something which, steaming, sends forth an odour most agree- ' able to the olfactory sense. As they more away the watcher understands that Charles Lamb's description of the origin ot roast pig is not so far fetched as he at one time supposed, Before long however the hue and cry is raised. The bares are in full fight with the hounds—in the shape of a gallant sergeant—hotly in pursuit. In lees time than one can tell the tale he had run his quarry to earth and they stand self confessed—the purloiners of a boiled ham Who played the most important part you ask? Not the ham because it was not eaten and that was all it was fit for. Not the men because they could not keep it when they had it which was all they were fit for. 1 herefore the sergeant —brave fellow—was the mon important personage for he collared his men which is princi- pally what he was fit for.

The Halton-road Sunday school scholars are to be pitied Last year at one or two of the socials some Of the juveniles—and perhaps adults—engaged in the heinous crime of osculating, which by interpre- tation means that they had the audacity and un- blushing effronteri to play "kissing-ring." The effective carrying out of this naughty game so horrified the powers that be, that when this dread- ful thing came to their ears, they visited their just wrath in condign punishment upon the heads of the devoted scholars. Henceforth there would be no more socials at Halton-road and it is now stated that the officials memories are EO retentive that they do not intend to allow any socials to be held in connection with the schools this winter at the Church or Sunday school. If this be true the scholars will be well advised if they will revolt from such a tyrannical mandate and holdtbeir annua socials elsewnere. •An yet the Church asks why it cannot maintain control over the youths and maidens ! !

The tenders for the constructiun of this bridge across the Mersey at an estimated cost of £120,000 have been agreed upon. The Arrol's Roof and Bridge Company, Germiston, Glasgow,lonve secured the contract for the steel lattice and iron work, while that for the masonry has been placed with Messrs Thornton and Sons, Wellington-drive, Liver- pool. Preparations are in progress for making the approaches to the bridge on the Runcorn bank of the river,this work being in charge of Mr J Weston who built the celebrated Weaver sluices for the Ship Canal. Tie transporter bridge is expected to be completed in 18 months. ANNUAL CONCERT—VICTORIA ROAD BOARD SCHOOLS. .

MAGNETISM IN A BOILED HAM. That a newly boiled brown crumbed barn possesses magnetic properties was amply demon- strated at the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning when two disreputable middle-aged men named Henry Kehhaw, alias Henry Shaw, Halifax, and William Johnson, flatman, were charged with stealing a boiled ham valued at 14s on the 3rl inst., the property of Thomas Saunders, provision mer- chant, Church-street. Tke evidence showed that on the date mentioned Mr Saunders placed the ham, almost steaming hot, on the counter at 9.30 p.m. Hearing a slight noise in the shop, he glanced through the kitchen window but could see no one. Obviously, however, the two prisoners had been attracted by the aromatic smell emanating from the luscious joint, for on coming into the shop half an hour later he found the boiled ham missing. Saunders, like a sensible fellow, at once informed the police, and thanks to the energy of SerZeaut Gregory the two prisoners were apprehended within a few minutes of the theft. Knowing where the delinquents were likely to be that officer made his way to a lodging-house in Friar-street and found Johnson coming into Church-street with the ham under his arm. Questions elicited the fact that Johnson had received the ham from Kershaw with instructions to sell it, and that the ham had already been offered to a Martin Gilligan and a Mrs Hunt, who had, however, declined to have anything to do with the joint, though it had been proffered them at the nominal figure of is 61. Both prisoners, when charged at the police station, admitted the theft, and as they were nld convicts they were commituted for trial to the Knutsford Quarter Ses- sions. Ought not Runc-or-n---ic;- be proud of Mr W E Hougb,who on Saturday played such a magnificent garni for Cheshire against Lancashire? Runcorn

5

hitherto, has been known as the breeding ground of Rugby playing youths, but now the town of ships, soap, an chemicals will be noted for having produced an Association player who may, ere long. figure in first-class fixtures. Of course everybody was pleased when it was known that W Hough was picked to represent his county in the struggle at Chester against Lancashire, Unfortunately, how- ever, the honour does not attach itself to Runcorn as directly as one would have wished, owing to lielsby having secured the services of this player. but, nevertheless, Runcorn bred him, and it is to this town alone that he owes his present proficiency. Runcorn, therefore, may justly claim a share of the honour, the more so when it is known that he lives and works in Runcorn, but plays as association goal-keeper for fielsby.

The " Athletic News" speaking of the game be- tween Cheshire and Lancashire says :—" Grim is a word insufficient to describe the struggle at Ches- ter on Saturday, where amateur elevens represent- jog Cheshire and Lancashire met in combat after a lapse of a considerable number of years. Though the pace at whici the game opened was remarkably slow, the work of the amateur undoubtedly being apparent, play ruled even unil 25 minutes had elapsed, when Hough the Cheshire custodian,earned three or four cheers for his excellent work. The marvel of itsll was how, after his backs were beaten, and the white-robed Lancastrian forwards were upon him like a pack of wolves, he got the ball away. His display came as an eye-opener,and it was only to be expected that defeat would come his way sooner or later. A hot shot, close in, from Sykes was caught by the little man, after the manner a wicket keeper takes the ball, and fumbling it, the leather pas3ed into the net, greatly to Hough's chagrin. That the Lancastrian victory of four goals to three was lucky in the extreme cannot be gainsaid. To snatch a win 13 seconds off time was running it close, and the result chagrined the Cheshire crowd not a little. Hough of Helsby, an obscure village, which has an excellent local eleven, was undoubt- edly the saviour of his side. But for his cat-like agility and marvellous precision in punching and kicking, a score of eight goals, aye, ten, might have been run up against the Cestrians. Hough is just the sort of lad a First League team would like to discover, and if he does not makethis way into first-class football I sha.l be mistaken."

--- It is not often that " Cestrian" writes in this strain about one who comes from an " obscure village," ani, therefore, very critically written we can all the more readily believe that " the little man " not only enhanced his already high local re- putation but bestowed upon the town an honour from which it would not willingly dissociate itself.

By ANOTHER CONTRIBIIT3II.. How are the: mighty fallen! Strange. isn't it that a good club often goes under when it is sup- posed to have an easy thing on. Everybody who is anybody in football predicted a victory for Run- corn on Saturday. It was only a Case of how many points Huddersfield would have to suffer from the Cheshire Champions. What a rude awakening it was to Runcornians when the telegram came, Huddersfield 20; Runcorn, 5; and vouched for by the familiar name of Ellison. Those who had not seen the telegram would not believe the news. But it was too true. What excuses canlavail in face of such a thorough thrashing. It is not a case of penalty goals and ifluky drop kicks. Runcorn's lines were crossed no fewer than six times and memory tails to recall how many years it is since the defence of the Green-uns broke down so completely. The absence of Houghton was no doubt a great factor in pav- ing the way for deleat. for not only did the team miss his personality, but the three-quarter line was sadly disarranged. It was forward, however, that the weakness lay and considering how the men have keld their own so far, this is somewhat stir- prisinz, but perhaps they are feeling the effscts of toeir hard work, and besides I do not think the best eight for such a tussle were chosen. But na amount of excuses or complainings will alter the fact that the hitherto vaunted superiority of Lan- cashire over Yorkshire fo Absll received a severe shock on Saturday, for Runcorn were not the only club to bite the dust badly.

TRH WARRINGTON BURGLARY.

Georwe Chapman,l 19. described as a fisherman, pleaded guilty to three indictments —(I) charging him with burglariously break'ng and entering the dwelling house of John Cosgrove, at Warrington, on November 24. and stealing a watch a gold ring, and other property, and £1 17s in money ; (2) that he burglariously broke into and entered the dwelling house of Lharles H Badger at Whiston,on November 29,and stole property to the value of £4 10s tend (3)that;he broke into Mr Badger's wate- house on the same dale, and stole several articles. He was found dealing with the property In St Anne-street, Liver- pool, on the following day. His record was a bad one. He had broken out of the home to which he had been se,t, and was dealt with as a refractory pauper. He was committed to jail for three years.

A CIVIL DEBT AND CRIMINAL PROCEEDINGS.

Ralph Plant, 24, potter, was charged with. at Warrington on April 6 and 9, 19C03 defrauding by false pretences William Breward and inducing him to accept three bills of exci.ange for the payment of the respective sums of £.4,233. and £3l 17s. Mr Harrison prosecuted, and Mr Rutledge de- fended.—ltiwas alleged that the bale were given in respect of a representation that certain crates of pottery consigned to Breward were lying at Longport Railway Station,whereas when Breward went to the station on a date arranged there were no such goods there. It trauspired in evidence that several crates of pottery were deposited at the station on the dates mentioned by the prisoner. Breward admitted re- ceiving several crates and sending back seven which came to hand as useless. Prisoner went into the box and swore that had his firm not gone into bankruptcy he would have executed all the orders he had from Breward. The landlord put the bailiffs in possession of the premiees. and the goods in question were afterwards realised by the official receiver in bankruptcy.—The Chairman intimated that he did not think the case was sufficiently strong to conviet, and the jury agreed with him. The Chairman said it was an attempt made to recover a civil debt by criminal proceedings—a prac- tice which was to be deprecated.—The jury found the prisoner not guilty, and he was discharged.

EARLY WARRINGTON LITERATURE.

Interesting Lecture by Mr. Madeley.

Early VVa rington printed books " was the suiej-ct of a highly interesting lecture aelivered at tbo Museum on Thursday last week, under the auspices of the War riugton Society by Mr C Madeley. Councillor Berms t presided,but,no doubt owing to the state of the weather there was a small attendance. At the outset the lecturer said the local collection is the: Library was of considerable extent, comprising about 2,000 books and pamphlets. If they dealt with Warrington books broadly they would have to begin their :survey at the year 174 as that was the date of the earliest book they hal which had any connection with Warrington. Teat book was edited by a Warrington man and printed in Italy. He proposed to deal only with early Warrington printed books, and would therefore have co brain about 300 years later, and stop at about thp year 1504. Toe local codsction in the library had been principally derived from two sources, one a large collection of books and pamphlets which were received be gift and bequest from Dr Kendrick, and the other a number of books which were purchased by means of subscriptions; contributed for about ten years by a number of friends of the library at a time when the Committee did not feel justified, on account ore the smallness of their income, in purceasing the books nut of the ordinary funds. Mr Madeley then went cr, to say that John Eyres was the first Warrington printer whose name they had, and his name appeared at the foot of a broadside, which was the earliest piece of Warrington printing they were aware cf, the date of its pruductiou being about 1734. The first piece of Warrington literature which be (Mr Madeley) had come acrots was " The import- ance of Is arning," a sermon preached before an assembly of ministers in Manchester in 1760,the imprint being sin' ply"lVarriogton." The printing of the famous William Ears was dealt with,and the lecturer mentioned that Ey res was closely conureted with the Library being in his early days the librarian. Mr Madeley pro-, ceedel to speak of the tremendous influence exercised on the production of literature and printing in Warrington by the Academy which was founded in 1757. Apparently the first man conn, cted with the Academy who 'leashed or had anything printed at Warrington was 'Dr Priestley, who came here in 1761. The works of the other famous writers cooneoted with the Academy were touched upon, the names of D: Forster, I Dr, Enfield, the Aikin family. Mrs Barbauld and I Gilbert Wakefield being mentioned. Li referring to Eafield's bock ' The Speaker " Mr Madeley said it was at one time as well known in England as any book at all except the Bible. It served a great purpose and did a great deal of good, and it was a epoestioa whether the coming down to the popular taste which had lately taken place in this class of book. resultea in such real advantage to the student as the study of Eeficld's " Speaker." Continuiog, Mr Madeley said that in Eome respects the greatest name on their list was that of John Howard, the prison reformer, who came to Warriegton in order to get his great work on the state of prisms in England and Walni,printed. It was rather the fashion o say that Howard's reform work was a failure, and did not produce any effect in the reform of prisons, bet still, as an inspiration for other reformers, the work which Howard did and his actual personal life had been of the very greatest value. A magnificent book printed in Warring:trio:was the "Memoirs of the Earls of Warren and Surrey," by the Rev John Watson. of Stockport. Other works referred to were those of the Rev Edward Owen, Rector of Warrington, Rev James Glaz3brook, Vicar of Si James's, wtii.oh comprised sermons and productions of a devotional character. Mr Madeley read a number of controversial pamphlets issued with respect to the pro- posal to establish a " Court of Conscience " at Warring- ton and do away with certain butchers' stalls. One of the pamphlets asserted that if the proposals were carried out " the beautiful Bridge-street would be deserted and overgrown with grass." (Laughter.) The last work referred to was the evie poem " Alfred "by John Fitchett, printed by Haddock, Warrington, Eyres' successor. Mr Madeley said it was about the longest poem ever written in the English language, containing about 10 times as many lines as an ordinary poem. When be was asked if the poem was read in Warring- ton he replied" No ! We are satisfied with having pro- duced it." (Laughter.) All the works referred to by Mr Madeiey were exhibited on a table and were afterwards inspected with tee greatest interest. One of the most unique of these was the manuscript of the poem " A'fred." The lecturer was cordially thanked on the proposition of Mr H Stuart Page, seconded by Mr E J Hall.

SKATING ACCIDENT IN CANADA,—A skating party from Government House broke through the ice on the Ottawa river. Miss Blair, the daughter of the Minister of Railways, and Mr Albert Harper, Assistant Deputy Minister of Labour, who went to the rescue, were both drowned.

B;RD SHOW AT WARRINGTON.

A Successful Event.

A succsesful show wag held in Holy Trinity schools on Saturday under the auspices of the Warrington Fanciers, Association. The show was the sixth annual event of its kind which has taken place in connection with this Association and the exhibits consisted of canaries, mules, British arid for2ign bins and pigeons. The show was in every way a SUCC.S9, and was considered to be the best ever seen in Warrington. Mr A Hirst, of Accrington, was the judge of the cage birds, and his awards gave every satisfaction. The pireens were in the hands of Mr Aehcroft, of Moseley, wh o displayed capable judgment. The entries in the cage bird classes showed a Isrge increase over last year, there bin v 331 as against 219 previously. In the open classes Messrs R Barker and El Cawley showed birds of exceptional merit the former winning no fewer than five special pezes including the handsome set of carvers given by Messrs Lsver Bros, l'irt Sunlight, and also the Presi- dent's prin. The entrim in the pigeon classes showed a Wight detrease.but what was lacking numerically was amply made up for in quality, Some excellent birds from Messrs Burrows, Gould, Leigh and Bloor being on view. Mr Gould, Lymm, carried off the President's prizs. The executive worked indefatigably' to ensure the SUCCESS of the exhibition, ant, praise should be accorded to the President, Mr W W Leigh, Messrs God- win and Boyle, sec,etaries, and Mr J D Leigh, treasurer for their efforts The show was visited by a large num- ber of pecple dzring the day. WARRINGTON. Children's Concert in Parr Hall.

The annual concert in connection witn Silver- street Wesleyan day school was held in Parr Hall on Tuesday evenirg, and was attended with com- plete success. The children composing the chorus, who were effectively grouped on the platform. opened the concert by singing "Float, is sounds" (J Beschnitt), and later they sang " The wanton winds play "(F Abt) in a very creditable manner. The infants sang a very pretty action song, " The merry little drillers " (with musical bell drill,) and were warmly applauded. Miss S Silcock suc- cessfully sang " Soldiers in the Park." from "The runaway Girl," and "The toy Monkey," from "The Geisha," A rainbow action song by the junior girls was a very acceptable item, while a floral dance and song proved even more attractive, and an intricate and pretty scheme was very ably carried through. Mr Tom Gunson won high en- comiums by his rendering of "Mary of Argyle," and in response to an enthusiastic encore gave "Killarney." The song of the river" (HA Donald) was very charmingly sung by one of the children, Miss J Webster, and the applause which followed was very cordial. During an interval the distribu- tion of prizes took place. Mr RE Garnett, who piesided, delivered a brief address. After alluding to the attention the Wesleyan Church paid to the important question of education, he said the ques- tion would probably in the near future be one of the most vital questions to come before the coun- try. The speaker phid a tribute to the good work done at Silver-street schools and congratulated Mr Upson, the head master on the results attained. Mr Garnett promised to defray the cost of the hire of Parr Hall, which is about £6 Mrs Garnett distributed prizes to about 150 chil- dren. The entertainment was resumed. Mr Tom I Gunson sang "In Old Madrid." The senior girls gave a song, dance and tableaux,"Hindoo Maidens," the chorus "Ten little Chinamen " (C. T. West), and the senior scholars an action piece. " The song of the Union Jack," and the senior boys a song, Idialogue, and dance, "A night in a Dormitory." Toe concert reflected considerable credit upon Mr. John Upson kthe head master) and his competent staff of assistants. The acsompanists were Miss Lewis, Miss B Lewis, and Miss Woolacott. Mr Ernest Wright accompanied for Mr Tom Gunson.— We have been requested to state that the Mayor was asked to take the chair, but that an important engagement in Herefordshire prevented him doing so. He,boever, sent two guineas towards the school funds.

PRESENTATION TO EX-SERGEANT DEVLIN.

There was an interesting ceremony in the Board Schad's, Great Sankey. on Tuesday evening, when a presentation of a purse of gold was made to ex-Sergeant Devlin, who has recently retired from the LAnctsbire County Cons:abulary atter a long term of useful sec- vice.

On the motion of Mr Halton, seconded by the Rev J R Jones, Mr George Thompson was voted to the chair, and also asked to make the presentation. Mr Thompson said be felt it a compliment to have been selected to make the presentation became he had taken considerable interest in getting up the testi- monial. lie knew the ex-sergeant very well and had always found him up to his work. The purse, which had the name of toe ex-Sergeant inscribed upon it, con tamed £8 101.

The Sergeant was then called into the room, after which the Chairman called upon the Rev J R Jones as the Vicar of the parish, and who, he said, they expected to lead them in matters of this sort. The Rev J R Jones remarked that tie was glad to be present to show his appreciation of the public services which Mr Devlin had rendered to the community, net so muce as Sergeant Devlin, but as a functionary of the law, without which order would soon be lost. They who went to bed comfortably and qaietly at night, failed to appreciate as they ought the fact tbat they could not enjoy their comfortable snores and, get up to breakfast the next morning and not find their houses broken into uel .se someone had been watching all night and it was to ex-Sergeant Devlin and his contreres that they were indebted tor such peaceful nights. Ile was sorry they had not more money to present to him, but he must remember that the value of the money was not the full expression of the feeling of the neiehbourtood towards him. (Hear, bear.) Mr Halton also voiced the feeling of the inhabitants with regard to the esteem in which the sergeant was held for his serve es to the district during the time be had been stationed in it. He (Mr Haltoo) had always I found him anxious te do his duty and to further the interests of those who employed him. Hie duties must have been very difficult, in a district of this description as it was so wide and se itteree, and now that the population of the district was increasing he thought an additional police-officer should be provided to cepa with the growing difficulties. There had been many robberies in Warrington and the district of late, and as they had heard of similar robberies at Prescot and Liverpool it was clear that the thief or thieves were travelling in their district. They bad the assurance of the Superintendent that the police arrangements of the districts were to be remodelled and they must await results. In conclusion Mr Halton expressed the hope that Mr Devlin would live long and not forget the time he bail spent in Sankey. Mr J Fulton Smith said he endorsed all that bad just been said by the Vicar and Mr Halton. They certainly owed a great deal to the police service in Sankey. They were peculiarly situated in this parish as it was a high- way between two very important towns, and they were visited by that portion of the population whose absence they preferred to their presence and who were a source of annoyance now and agate. But in this neighbour- hood they bad been wonderfully exempt from any trouble in that direction, and he put mat down to DI activity of the police. (Hear, hear.) The Clerk said that as an offieial in the district 60 would like to state that he had received the greatest assistance from Mr Devlin. ' Tbe Chairman in making the presentation remarked that notwithstanding the hard weather he had had to brave, he was glad to find the sergeant lookieg so well. They all hoped that tie and his family would be long spared and have every happiness. Their oely regret was that the response to their appeal bad not been taken up throughout the district as it ought to have been, the chief portion of the money having come from Sankey and Buttonwood. There had been one subscriber from Penketh and two from Cuerdley. Mr Thompson then handed the puree to Mr Devlin. , Mr Devlin acknowledged the gift in suitable terms and remarked that he bad always found the people of the district loyal, and he Lai had no difficulty in discharg- ing his duties. The people bad given him every assistance when a crime had been committed Usually, the crimes committed in the district were by strangers and he bad always received the greatest ' assistance from the inhabitants. He wished to acknow- ledge the assistance he had always received from Mr Smith in the compilation of statistics and reports relat• ing to the district. This terminated the proceedings.

Xetterl to the Ebitor.

Corporation Finance. Fir,—What astonishes me with regard to the Corpor- ation bank overdraft is the fact that amongst all the Councillors and Officials not a single individual Ns come forward to offer a word of explanatica. Why is the public kept in the dark ? The figures are in themselves positively alarming, as in two years viz :—from March 1399 to March 1901, the liability of the Corporation to the Manchester and Liverpool District Bank, increased from £14,163 15310 i to £122,894 9i 4d, an increase of £103,725 13s 61. Is thes.e no one who will relieve the minds of the rate- payers ? The Bank Rate U.-day is 4 per cent. What are we paying for this large overdraft ? Yours truly, WILLIAM DOOLEY, Bewsey-road Warrington, December 6tb,

An Orford-lane Complaint.

Sir,—Would you kindly permit me to ask our repre- sentatives of Sr. John's and Orford wards, also the Lighting Committee, if we are to continue throughout this winter as we were last, in regard to the darkness of this busy thoroughfare and still be content with the premise" It shall have cur attention." This is not sufficient, what we want is immediate attention, seeing that the lamp taken down for the rebuilding of the " Wheat Sheaf Hotel " places the neighbourhood worse than before. Only on Wednesday evening a child was accidentally run over near this spot,and as the authorities know this neighbourhood is a little rowdy, it is not right Lo leave i so neglected. Again as a victim to the uninvited vi,,itor or visitors with loss of and damage to property, I can assure your readers there is just cause for alai m. It would therefore greatly add to our safety and comtort if we had better light and police patrol. Yours respectfully, EDWARD PLINSTON. 1, Orfcrd-laneXarrington.

Runcorn School Board.—Canon Wood's Last Explanation.

Sir—The latest effusion of the Rev. Canon Wood in his official organ aud re-published in last week's " Examiner," is a pitiable exhibition of a strong man striving against what he knows to be the right and true course to pursue, and, in consequence, floundering deeper into the mire. His explanation is not just to his opponents. It is not true in substance nor in fact. ." Directly I knew it," he says, " I hastened to say that the fourpence by which the School Board rate rose from 1896 to the latter half of 1893 was not all duet) the transfer of the Wesleyan Schools." Directly be knew it ! How does that statement accord with the truth. During the '93 election the Progressive candidates issued a hand bill contradicting the statement, and pointing out that the increase was nearer 21. But the Canon and his party continued to " think " it was 4d and maintained their figures before the electorate. At a Board meeting a fort- night before the election just ended, Mr Norman again brought the subject before the Canon's notice, in his capacity as Chairman of the Board and told him the figures were not true. Canon Wood maintained they were true, and one of his first acts after the campaign opened, was to issue the old falsehood under a somewhat new guise. His statement that the question was never raised at the Board, and had laid dormant since the last election does not therefore accord with the fact. Canon Wood was in possession of the official documents ' and the most superficial inquiry would have shown him he was in the wrong. But it suited be and his party to continue to think the increase was 4d. It was not until he had the challenge from Mr Norman to submit the figures to a chartered accountant that the Canon left toe thinking stage. Then—so quickly did he find out his error—in a few hours he reduced the 4el to 3d. This was strange conduct for one so precise, so exact, and such a great stickler for order as Canon Wood. And what happens after this ? Is the error corrected by handbill in the same way it had been circulated far and wide ? No. Is any honourable amendment made? No. The offence is still further aggravated in a way that the Canon's pleas that he " thought" and that he was " careless " and that no one would suppose that he had " purposely said what he knew not to be correct " that these pleas can by no means extenuate. The lie --that is the true definition which the circumstances warrant—the lie was fepeated on the very morning of the polling day. A handbill headed " Wednesday morning " issued so late that no effective reply could be given the electors was spread broadcast. This handbill stated " After all the fact remains that the rate was 51 before the Wesleyan Schools were closed and now is 9d and has beer, for years." The Canon's own phrase " strategy of truth aptly describes this " fact." Can Canon Wood wonder that be and his party should be attacked when they play the high game of education so low down as this ? This is not hastening to repair error, is it? There is no qualification of " thought" about this. The statement does not even bear an aspect of being careless. This handbill was issued nearly a fmrtnight atter the Canon had publicly reduced the 41 to 3d. I leave ratepayers to draw the inferer ce frorr this brief history of the controversy. It is due to the Progressive members of the School Board that the public should be placed in possession of facts so diame- trically opposed to the statement published by the Canon in his own magazine and given a wider circula- tion through the local Press. It will be a sorry day for England when it becomes general for those who are leaders of public thounht and public action to prostitute truth, honesty and justice for the sake of party advan- tsge.—Yours truly, IMPERIALIST.

The Concentration Camps.

is amazing how many survivals of barbarism one sti'l meets—cr learns of—in the midst of civilisa- tion. One of the most depressing at the present time, is the primitive form of usage adopted by the present Goverrment with regard to women and children are congregated together in!lvrhat are termed the concen- tration camps of South Africa. To people professing Christian principles in this England of ours ft is be- coming more and more imperative that some steps should be taken with a view of compelling the Govern- ment or powers that be to at least mitigate the rr isery which we learn is now reaching so deplorable a condi- tion indeed. One wonders with Burke whether the age of chivalry is gone and if that of sophistets and calcu- lators has succeeded, for it would appear that the nurse of manly sentiment is a thing of the past and that the glory of England has almest become xtinguished, were it not that in several parts of the country there have already been brought to bear many resolutions by citizens of different towns denoting in a most emphatic manner their disapprobation felt for the system now so unhappily in vogue with respect to concentration camps in South Africa. It may reason- ably be conjectured that in the course of a week or two the town of Runcorn will once again be the ar na for Chrirtmas carolling when the glad tidings of peace on earth and good will to all men will be earnestly voiced by the choirs of the respective Churches connected therewith. La view of the existing matters referred to ' above, will it notf appear somewhat incongruous during Christmas-tide if nothing is done in Runcorn by our , ministers of religion in the way of bringing about a meeting, the objects of which will be to emphasize in unmistakeable teims, the necessity there is, that meas- ures shall be adopted with a view of bringing about a more congenial state of affairs in these camps, and one which will be characteris:ic of so renowned a country as England for liberty and justice to mankind. The thr es demand that steps should be taken by ministers of the Gospel, for at is astounding to think or even to realise, that the mortal- ity among the women and children exceeds even the hiehest death rate of any of the large towns of Great Britain. We learn from given statistics that it is even proportionably higher than that which prevailed dur- ing the plague in Bombay and which during last September reached the abnormal rate of 565 per thousand. Mine is not the task to paint the gloomy horrors of the tomb. Such were the words of the Scottish Port Blair, in his "Elegy on the Grave"—written some two hundred years ago—but we may take his dictum to heart on friendship—that friend- ship the mysterious cement of the soul and sweet solder of society—is still as great a factor in bringing about:that state of fellow feeling %bid] would enable one to be at least kir d to his fellow mortals and earth- born companions. To the ladies of Runcorn I would also entreat that they will do their utmost at the various meetings which are invariably held in connection with the respective Churches. Much can be done by formu- lating resolutions and much will be assuredly done in this respect when it becomes universally known that of the women incarcerated in these camps there is not one. whose youngest child is now living. It will be seen that this question appeals with special force to the women of this country to exert themselves in trying to alleviate their sex as much as possible by con- vening meetings, in which the sterner sex shall partici- pate and shall be conducted by the ministers of the Free Church Council. If needs be a puLlic meeting might be held to endorse in no uncertain manner the resolutions already sent up for the purpose of alleviating and ameliorating the condition of women and children who are unnecessarily suffering through the stern reali- ties of war. Hoping the matter will be taken up, as is suggested. lam yours truly, Runcorn. J. Mc L.

Great Chemical Works Development. It was officially intimated on Thursday morning that Messrs Gossage and Sons, soap manufacturers, Widnes. together with a number of other in- fluential manufacturers, have acquired a site of 141 acres at Rudheath, Northwich, and are abut to erect a large works for the manufacture of chemicals and by-products. The essential brine has been found. The site has a frontage to the North Stafford Canal and the London and North-Western Railway. It is understood that the manufactory will require a large number of hands. It will pro- tably be worked in conjunction with Gossage's Soap Works, which use large stocks of chemicals.

THE WAR

. LATEST TELEGRAMS. Ihe Capture of the Bethel Commando:

PRETORIA. TUESDAY.

The capture of 131 Boers yesterday morning at Trecbardsfontein, 15 miles west of Bethel, by General Bruce-Hamilton is announced. A laager was taken and seven Boers killed. Further details are not yet to hand. The prisoners would belong to Wolmaran's ccmmando, 300 strong, which after remaining a few days north-west of Bethel in tou2n with another force of the samo Etrength broke at the beginning of the week, Wolmaran's coming south.

At the Head of our Troops.

Writing at Pretoria on Tuesday Mr Bennett Burleigb, the war correspondent of the "Daily Telegraph," says : —Lord Kitchener, now that General Sir lan Hamilton is here, has taken the field in person and is directing the movement cf our troops.

LABOUR REPRESENTATION.

Mr. S. Woods Proposed as Candidate

for Newton Division.

Lancashire miners bays aeon given practical effect to the scheme of Labour representation which has recently been decided upon throughout the area of the Miners' Federation of Great Britain. Upon the basis which is suggested there may be thirty-four or thirty-five candi- dates put up at the next general election under the scheme, counties being entitled to nominate one candi- date for every ten thousand members. Lancashire's quota will thus be four, but as yet only one name has teen before the county conference—viz., Mr S Woods, president of the Federation,whose suggested candidature for the Newton Division at the next election was ap- proved at the monthly gathering of the Federation dele- gates at Wigan on Saturday. 'lt is, we understand, a condition that candidates must reside in the county where the division is they are ,run for. Mr Woods as is well-known, is not a new hand, having sat in Parlia- ment for Ince and Walthams tow ; and it is doubtless intenled as a mark of recognition of his past services and of the position he at present holds as president of the county Federation and secretary to the Parliament- ary Committee cf the Trades Union Congress that the honour of being the first name submitted to the general body of miners as a candidate for a seat in Parliament under the new scheme has been conferred upon him. Mr Woods has resided in the Newton Division about thirty years, and it is estimated that the mining vote will be a little less than two-di lairds of the whole of the electors.

_ It is probable four Labour candidates may be run under the scheme in Lancashire when the rime. ccmeß, and if so, the other three constituencies will be selected, doubtless, from ths following—viz., Ice, Wigan, St Helens, Westhough.ton, Radeliffe-tium-Farnworth,E3c'es and L!igh.

EARLESTOWN WORKING MEN'S FLYING CLUB.

Annual Dinner.

The annual dinner of the E arles town Working Men's Flying Club was held at the Oddfellown' Arms I.,2gh- street, on Saturday eight. Over 701 members and friends sat down to an excellent repast, prepared in his usual excellent style by Mr Lythgoe, the host. The meal having been much enjoyed, an adjournment was made while the tatles were cleared. The after proceed- ings were presided over by Mr George Gay (president of the Liverpool and District Federation), Who was sup- ported by Messrs F Gregson (president of the city of Liverpool Federation), T Southern (president of the club), Mr Charles Appleton. and others. In the absence of his brother through illness, Mr Charles Appleton then read the third annual report, which stated that the season had in every way been a great success, and he might say that the Federation to which they belonged bad in no small way contributed towards that desirable result. The programme was an extensive one, inciuding five races for old birds and five races for young birds. It was carried through without one single mishap, which spokeeloguently for the man- agement of the St Helens and District Federation, The membership of the society continued to increase. Last year the number was 47, this year they had 52, and in ev ,ry o her bruin's the increase was well maintained. For instance, in their firit year they raced 336 birds, this year they sent to all races 1,437 birds, a splendid in crease over last year of 1,101. The amount paid in pezes (not including specials) totalled £56 12s, an in- crease over last year of £22 14e. The principal winners were Messr, J Kitts £8 Sc, including thtee-guinea cup. W Conner £7 163, Jickson Bros. £.6 us, Lythgoe and Son £5 4s, including three-guinea cup, and J Lovrsay £3 15s 6d. The income' for the year was £lO3 lOs 61, and the expenditure £96 is 21, leaving a balarce in 'hand of £7 9i 4cl. In conclusion the committee desired to thank the special prize giver; :—Messrs Jolley,!Greg- ton, Lythgne and Son, Bibbed, Woods, Jackson Bros., astley, King and several other gentlemen. Personally e wished to thank the'r respected President for the great amount of assistance the Secretary had received from him, In his estimation a harder working president did not exist. He also wished to thank the committee, who had, on all occasions, worked together satisfactorily. The Chairman then distributed the prizes and dip- lomas, which had been won by the members. The chief prizes were those mentioned in the secretary's report,Mr T Southern (president) said he was pleased to be present and to see such a splendid gathering. The club was now in its third year. In their first year the society only numbered 24 members. That was not bad fora start. They flew their old birds up to Bournemouth and young birds to Swindon. In the second year the mem- bership was 52, the old birds being flown to Jamey and the young b rds to Swindon. They had 52 members at the present time. They flew their old birds to Nantes and their young birds to Bournemouth in the any age race. They had also two members who had successfully flown Bordeaux. They all knew very well how their club was commenced They only paid a small entrance fee of 7s 61 per mem- ber, and it spoke highly of the club and its members when they could give five pens with £2 for the first prize out of such a small entrance fee. They were not afraid to produce or publish their report so that every- one could see how they were situated. He had not seen a secretary in Lancashire who could he compared with their secretary. (Applause.) Mr Appleton had done his utmost for them curing the last few years, and be (the speaker) was sorry that he was not amongst them. A number of toasts were honoured and the remainder of the evening was spent in a convivial manner.
