EARLESTOWN & NEWTON

P.S:A. 1 The usual weekly meeting of the Earlestown and District P.S.A. Society, was held in the Co- operative Hall, on Sunday afternoon. Mr J Smith presided, and an interesting address was given by Mr J Parr. Vocal selections were given by Miss L B Parr, and recitations by Miss Newton. Miss Williamson presided at the piano. THE PENRHY.N CHOIR AT EARLESTOWN. On Monday evening the Town liall at Earles- town was packed to its utmost capacity on the occasion of a concert given by the Penrhyn Choir from Bethesda, the proceeds being in support of the locked-out quarrymen. The arrangements had been carried out by a committee composed ot the Earlestown Trades Council and the Eisteddfod Committee with Mr W Speakman as secretary. Dr Latham officiated as chairman and at the outset referred to the fact that Lord Penrhyn happened to be the lessee for life of land which contained valuable mineral called slate, and it was a pure accident of birth that he was in possession of it. Parliamenc, had decreed that workingmen had a , right to combine for their mutual protection but Lord Penryhn had refused to meet the accredited representatives of the men and he also refuses the friendly effarts of arbitration by the Board of Trade. Evidently Lord Penrhyn had a very poor case or he would have been willing to submit it to the arbitration of his friends. The choir was working voluntarily to stay the icy hand of hunger, and the audience by their presence showed that they at all events intended that,the men,womenand children of Bethesda should have jusuice done to them. The musical items were then proceeded with and were selected from the extensive reper- toire of the choir. A couple of songs were given in a capital soprano voice by a youth named Gunson af Manchester who gave his services to the choir. A collection was made and ..C5 Is 4il realised. EARLESTOWN SHOP ASSISTANTS. On Sunday afternoon a meeting of the Shop Assistants' Organization, under the auspices of the local branch, was held at the Co-operative Hall, Wargrave-road, Earlestown. Mr Greerson, of Liver- pool. presided over a fair attendance. After the business of a routine character had been transacted, the Chairman referred to the advisability of engag- ing organizers to spread about the piinctples o?the association. He had seen a letter asking them to invest their funds in liability companies. He considered they would have a much more lucrative investment than that by putting on three new organizers. In twelve months it I would return them something like 40 per cent.— Ii was ultimately decided, on the proposition of Mr Snowball, seconded by Mr Davies, that the branches be recommended to consider the advisability of employing more organizers.—The Chairman said that afternoon he was going to preach the gospel of personal responsibility and was going to take as his text the position of the Earlestown branch. There must be a screw loose somewhere as they knew so little about Earlestown. He believed in that town there were something like 14,000 inhabit- ants,from which he inferred there were from 150 to 200 shopworkers. Out of that number they might reasonably expect from 80 to 100 to be organized. Not only was the number small, but it had been re- duced from 20 to 17. There was one point he was glad to bear of and that was the 17 members were tully alive to their position. The Chairman then dealt with various matters they had under consider- ation,and urged the necessity of all shop assistants joining their ranks.—The various reports were afterwards submitted and the meeting terminated with the usual votes of thanks being passed. EARLESTOWN.

[Br A CORRESPONDENT]

FOOTBALL.

The match between Earlestown and Workington on the Mesnes ground on Saturday provided one of the finest exhibitions ef football ever seen on the ground, the play all through, particularly during the first half, being of a very high standard, Earlestown won the toss and had the advantage of a strong wind during the first forty-five minutes. After tne homestara had paid a brief visit to the Workington goal, the nail was carried up the field by the visitors forwards, a foul against Leigh help- ing them nearer to the goal. The free kick was put out to the right and afterwards centred, Wood putting the leather pest Chew after five minutes play. Earlestown livened up after this and an al- most continuous fusilade was maintained on the vis tor!goal. A Shot from Dawson would certainly have scored had not Brown knocked the leather out with his hand. A penalty was of course awarded, and Settle had no difficulty in making the scores level. The pressure was kept up and twenty minutes after the start, a fast low shot from Bartley wal fumbled by the custodian with the result that both he and the ball were bundled into the net by Barlow and Leach. The third goal came just before the interal a lovely shot from Barlow defeating Pratt all the way. At the interval Earlestown were leading by three goals to one although if they had bad anything like reason- able luck they would have been four or five goals in front. The second half was mostly contested in a pouring rain which entirely spoiled the game. No more scoring was done, and the game therefore re- sulted in a victory for Earlestown by three goals to one.

The play of the locals seems to improve every time they turn out, and their record especially when operating on their own ground, will bear comparison with any other team in the League. ney have played five oaaches at borne and have won all the lot scoring 18 points as against five by their opponents.

Newton turned out twc teams on Saturday one meeting Tranmere Rovers in a combination match and the other visiting Adlington in a Wigan cup tie. After leading by two goals to nil at the inter- val Newton had to put up with a three to two de- feu', at the bands of Tranmere,whilst the other team were trounced to the extent of 5 goals to 1 at Adliogton.

FINANCIAL LAXITY Al WIDNES.

Report to the Town Council. The monthly meeting of the Widnes Town Council was held on Tuesday afternoon,the Mayor(Alderman W. Winwooi Gossage) presiding. With respect to the de- falcations in the borough accountant's departm.nt and the dismissal of the chief collector (Mr Charles Whitfield), the sub-committee appointed to examine the matter presented their report, of which the foLowing is a resumil :—Your sot-committee were appointed to make an investigation of the department and its working in April last, on the voluntary withdrawal of Drury, but more especially in regard to the books kept by Drury, whien resulted in his conviction and imprisonment. Acting under your furthr instructions, we investigated matters in connection with Whitfieh.'s accounts, which produced a profound conviction on the minds of your committee, the details of which were communicated to you, and which led you to unanimously ask Whitfield to resign. We would point out that you have continually relied upon the regular reports of the auditors, by which you were led to believe that the management of the depart- ment left nothing to be desired, but we regret to say our investigations have disclosed the fact that this was not the case. In conclusion, your committee cannot I absolve the chief accountant from all blame believing that the departmeat has been administered with some laxity, but think that this expression of opinion, associated with the changes now insti- tuted, notably the daily checking, and the relinquishing of the secretarial dutles of the technical instruction committee by Mr Hoyle, vti'l result man improved state of affairs. The committee recommended that the three vacancies in the staff should be filled after advertising, and that the following members of the staff be pro- moted :—Mr Doan Sankey to be chief clerk, Mr Frede- rick Teasdale to be water rental clerk, Mr George D Stead to be gas rental clerk, Mr David Martin to be : collector, and Mr William Price to he collector. _ _ .

Councillor Owenq (chairman of the finance committee) in moving the adoption of ths minutes, psid a high tribute t. 3 the ability and acumen which had been mani. fested by the chief accountant (Mr Samuel floyle)whilst he had been in the employ of the corporstim.—The minutes were duly spened.a. zonfi air-looted.

IMPORTANT TEST CASE AT RUNCORN. --- Prosecution of Swine Slaughterers.

For some time now the question of ths legality of slaughtering pigs at places other than the slaughter- house provided by the local authority has been agitating the minds ot Runcorn butchers and others who kilt pigs. It will lie remembered that some time ago the Runcorn Ct-opeffltive society made an application to the Urban Council to be permitted to kill pigs at the rear of the premises in Church-street. The application, after due consideration, was almost unanimously refused on the ground that a ceitain bye-law made it impossible to sanction private slaughter-houses. One or two members pointed out that there were scores if not hundreds of private individuals who were in the habit of killing pigs at the backs of their houses. As a preliminary step, therefore, a great number of—if not all— such persons were served with notices to the effect that they must desist or face the music of a public prosecu- tie% This- action by the Local Authority and its officials created something like consternation among a certain section of the community who have been follow- ing up this"honoured"(?)custom for over a score years, and quite a wave of resentment has been created by the firm and decided stand cf the " citizen fathers " who have not hesitated to summon before " beak and bench" the obstreperous members who have defied the Council's authority. This then may be said to be the" full and complete " history of the proceedings which were held at the Police Court on Monday before Mr ir J Norman and Mr J Ockleston. when David Pilgrim, Halton-road, Ann Cowap, Stonehills ; Mary Price, Halton-road ; and George Hughes, Crown Vaults, were summoned by Mr Percival, Inspector of Nuisances, acting on behalf of the 17 ban Council, tor killing pigs in places other than the . public slaughter houses. Mr Lake appeared on behalf of the Council, and Mr Knowles Widnes, represented the defendants. In opening the case, the defendants' solicitor said that his clerk only received instructions at 10 p m on Saturday night, and he, Mr Knowles, had only received word from his clerk at 730 that morning. It was a matter that should be thrashed out in the interests of the law and in the public interest of the town. These people had been singled out because they were members oa the Co-operative Society, and because they had sold pigs to the Society. He asked for an adjournment since they had only received the ummonses on Saturday morning, for be intended to ead up the law on the subject.—Mr Lake thought they sad had ample time to prepare as the defendants bad . received written and verbal nctice months ago that they would be prosecuted.—Mr Knowles retorted that a town authority should be the last one to take a mean advant- age. It had been done so as not to give the de- fendants a chance of defending themselves. —The Chairman: DD not let us have any irritation.—Mr Knowles : I am not irritated Continuing he said that this practice had been going on for the last twenty or thirty years daily and weekly and now that the matter had been taken up as a public matter by a public body it should be thrashed out with deliberation.—Mr Lake said he did not object if his friend avc.uld pay the costs of the adjournment.—Mr Knowles objected to this Suggestion and after a further passage of arms between the solicitors during which one accused the other of listening to "idle nonsense " the matter was adjourned for a week.

STOCKTON HEATH. -- STOCKTON HEATH AND THE CORPORATION TRAM WAY PROPOSALS. .At the meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council on Tuesday last week the Corporation Tra m way proposals were considered and on the proposition of Mr. Pemberton it was decided " that the Corporation be asked to supply plans on a larger scale, with the proposed width of the roads marked on them, and further that they supply any further information which may be desired by the Parish Council." It was agreed that a deputation wait upon the Clerk of the Cheshire County Council with a view of obtaining the views of that Council upon the matter. The following resolution was also passed :—" That this Council heartily support the Warrington Corporatio bringing their tram. ways into Stockton Heath upon equitable terms and conditions to the inhabitants of the district..'

PLEASANT PARAGRAPITS. VERY frequently circumstances alter kisses. THERE are times when we seem to have very little influence with ourselves. SHE "You are a conundrum." He: "But I hope you haven't given me up yet." TUE CRUELEST Yrr.—She : "My face is my fortune." He: "Well poverty is no disgrace." Sax: "You say you met your friend acci- dentally ?" He " Yes ; I fell in with him while skating." "DON'T you like to see a man strong enough to throw off trouble?" " Yes ; if he doesn't try to throw it off on me." A MAN'S IDEA OF IT.—He: "What an artistic home the Murglesons have?" He: " Yes; you can'tfind anything in it that you dare sit down on." A PRELIMINARY STAGE. —" What big words your sister uses, Harry I" " Yes ; she does when she's first introduced. She'll be all right after a while." Hits STATUS. —Little Elmer: "Papa, isn't a cynic a man who is tired of everybody?" Professor Broadhead: " Yes ; and of whom everybody is tired." HER INFERENCE. —Mr. Captain Braggington : "My husband was renowned on the tented field." Sally Gay: "Why, I didn't know he had ever travelled with a circus !" "Do you know a good tonic for nervous persons, Simpkins?" "No; what I want to find is a good tonic for people who have to live with them." "Why, George, you haven't smoked any of those cigars I gave you for a present !" Hubby: "No, dear, I haven't the heart to burn anything you gave me." "No, I shall never ask another to become my wife." "Oh, yes, you will. There are many nicer girls you might ask." "No; if you won't accept me, who would?" MRS. COBWIGGER : "Oh, Henry, you don't know .how bad I feel. Freddie stole two of the mince pies I made." Cobwigger : "Never mind, my dear; perhaps they won't kill him." THE IRISH LECTURER: "The superiority of the old architecture over the new is beyond question, for where will you find any modern buildings that have lasted as long as the ancient ones?" "JUST listen how they are pounding the floor with their feet," said the tragedian behind the curtain- " Yes," said the leading lady, " that is the stamp of public approval." "THAT elopement in high life was so romantic. They stole out—" " No, no, you mustn't say that; they belong to the best society. You should say They kleptomaniacked." MRS. MYLES : "I told Mrs. Styles I had heaped coals of fire on her head, and she looked worried." Mrs. Fyles : "What made her look worried do you suppose?" Mrs. Myles: "She didn't know whether they were on straight or not." MISTRESS: "Mary, have you got a young man in the kitchen?" Cook (under notice to leave): I have. An' I consider a young man in the kitchen is a jolly eight better than an old man in the drorin'-room. So there !" WALLACE: "There is nothing like matrimony to make a man appreciate the value of money." Ferry: "That so. A sovereign a man gives to his wife does look bigger to him than any other sovereign." ORATOR: "This country is now in the grasp of foreign financiers. But where is our deliverer? Is there a George Washington among us?" Coloured Party (rising) "Yes, sah ; hyah I be, salt." SMALL SOUTHERN Bo Y (politely) : "Please pass the 'lasses." Prim Northern Aunt : " You mean molasses, do you not?" Small Southera Boy: "How can I mean mo"lasses when I haven't had any?" 0 LIBERTY! the crimes that are done in thy name ! Particularly the Yankee crime of noe sounding the "r, ' which leave thy name " Libutty I" HER UNANSWERABLE ARGUMENT.--." That young man means well," said Mabel's father, "but he isn't intellectual. He doesn't think much." "Yes, he does," said Mabel, confidently. "He thinks a great deal of me." A QUESTION OF TlME.—Farmer (with wife and two children) : "How much fer tickets fer the young 'uns ?" Ticket Clerk: "Between five and twelve, half fare." Farmer: "Hang it ! Mandy, we'll hey tew wait till ter-morrer. It's half-past twelve now." THE PHRASE ACCEPTABLE.—" I know," pleaded the little bride, humbly, " that I make a good many grammatical errors." "They are nothing," said the young husband, "to those mother used to make." MAMMA to Edie, aged three and a half years, just home from his first morning at the kindergarten: Well, Edie, how did you like it? Edie: I didn't like it a bit. The teacher put me on a chair, and told me to sit there for the present, and I sat and sat, and she never gave me the present." Too LARGE.—Flossie : I don't care. I think Jack Townley is really mean." Annette: "Why, Flossie?" Flossie "He wrote to me from Florida, saying he had shot an alligator seven feet long, and said when he shot another he would have a pair of slippers made for me." "DOCTOR, I wish you wouid give me some'ehing for this cold in the head." " But, my dear sir, I don't wan't it. I wouldn't even take it as a gift." It was only the doctor's little joke, and he enjoyed it even if he did lose a patient because of it. He wasn't a man who paid his doctor bills very promptly, anyway. COMPOSER "I hope you like my new opera?" Critic: "Oh ! It's good enough in its way, and I dare say it will be performed after the works of Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven and Ileyerbeer are forgotten." Composer (delightedly) : " Really ?" Critic: "Yes ; but—not till then." A MANAGER, posing as an authority, went into a school one day with the intention of putting the fifth standard through their pacing in the geo• graphy of Europe. He began: "What is the capital of 'olland ? " " Capital H !" was the crushing rejoinder from the smart boy of the class. The manager did not pursue his geographical in- quiries further. "Iv is only right that I should tell you," she said, "that father has lost all." "Not all !" he exclaimed. "Ye,, all," she asserted. " No' "he said, firmly ; "not all. You are still left to him. I could not be so cruel as to add to his misfortunes. Tell him—tell him from me that my generosity impels me to leave him what little lies in my power." "WHAT appears to be the matter with your father?" inquired the doctor, as he hastily put his clothes on. " He's got the plumbago," replied the boy. "I think that's what maw says it is." " Pain in the small of the back, I presume," said the doctor. "No, sir ; he hain't got no small of the back. My paw weighs two hundred and eighty- four pounds. Miss PEERSEEKER : "Oh, baron, I would so much like to hear you tell again about how King Ludwig presented you with a decoration when you were a mere little shaver, and--" The Baron Barberosse. : " A leedle shaver ! I did not shave any one yen I was leedle. I did not learn my trade until "—recollecting himself—" dot is—l did not learn a trade at all.' NOT FOR HIS EARS.—" Sweet, do you love 1119 as well as you did this time yesterday?" tenderly murmured the young bridegroom, leaning towards her. "Rather better, I think, dear," softly answered the young bride. "Then I'm happier than I was yesterday," he rejoined; "and I didn't think it was possible." Whereupon the hard- featured old bachelor in the seat next to them changed his position vehemently. WIFE: "How in the world can you afford to buy those expensive eigrrs ?" Husband : "I don't buy them." "Dear me! You don't mean to say any friend of yours is rich enough to give you such cigars, do you !" "Wail, no ; not exactly. That Young man who has got so smitten with our daughter"—" Not more than she is with him." " Well, he's an agent or something for a big firm of cigar importers, and generally has his pockets full of their best samples. Well, after we go to bed and the lights are turned down, takes them out of his pockets and puts'em on the mantelpiece—to keep 'em from getting crushed, I suppose. Then when it comes to leaving, between , the desire to get out without making any noise at such a late hour and the pain of saying good. night to our daughter, he forgets all about them. I tell you, Mariah, our daughter has been a pretty heavy expense, but she's payin' for herself now."
